The Bantu expansion changed Africa. Scientists are still tracing how.
2026-11-07

The Bantu expansion changed Africa. Scientists are still tracing how.

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
It’s not easy to extract iron from its ore. But the peoples of the ancient city of Great Zimbabwe—the capital of a thriving kingdom in southern Africa between 1000 to 1700 AD—were masters. At sites near the city’s stone ruins, clusters of iron-smelting furnaces survive today as mounds of rubble. At Mashava, a village 25 miles northwest, fragments bear fingermarks of those who long ago smoothed the structures’ clay interiors. Some of the furnaces were nearly an adult’s arm span in width, with walls thick and strong to contain the heat of more than 2,100° Fahrenheit needed to smelt iron. Unlike other furnaces that required workers to force air into them with bellows, these were designed to suck air in, then out through a chimney, no human labor required. This would have made it significantly easier to produce the iron, which was fashioned into hoes, axes, and arrowheads. “The number and size of these furnaces, together with the use of natural-draft technology, indicate intensive and technologically sophisticated iron production,” says archaeometallurgist Ezekiel Mtetwa of Chicago’s Field Museum of Natural History, who has long studied the furnaces. Scientists are extremely interested in the peoples who created this and other iron-powered societies across eastern and southern Africa. Research points to an adaptable and innovative group from western Africa as the masterminds. Known as Bantu speakers, these communities began gradually expanding across the continent’s southern half, starting from 6,000 to 4,000 years ago. In doing so, they introduced a fundamentally new, sedentary way of life—characterized by farming, raising livestock, pottery-making, and metalworking—to regions previously dominated by hunter-gatherer communities. This migration transformed not only the region’s history and technology, but also its languages: Today, around 350 million Africans speak one of roughly 500 distinct Bantu tongues, from southern African Zulu to eastern African Swahili. And at least as many Africans—as well as many African Americans and Afro-Latinos—carry Bantu ancestry in their DNA. Reconstructing this expansion has been a tough puzzle, especially given the region’s sparse written historical records and limited research funding. But a unique blend of scientists—linguists, archaeologists, and geneticists—have teamed up to unearth clues about the deep origins of the Bantu-speaking people, their path across Africa, and their relationships with other cultures. Although much debate and uncertainty remain, little by little the work is uncovering one of history’s largest and longest human migrations. “It completely reshaped the demography in sub-Saharan Africa, and we are really interested in trying to understand it,” says population geneticist Sandra Beleza of the United Kingdom’s University of Leicester. The first evidence of the Bantu expansion came from observations of similarities among Bantu languages. They’re so alike, in fact, that when the Portuguese explorer Vasco da Gama sailed around the southern tip of Africa in 1497, a Portuguese crew member who had spent time in west-central Africa could reportedly communicate with locals in southeastern Africa. Linguists working in the 19th century started referring to the language family as Bantu, the plural of muntu, meaning “human being” in many Bantu tongues. Later linguists reasoned that the languages stemmed from an ancestral people who migrated across the continent from western Africa, which harbors the greatest diversity of Bantu languages (as would be expected for where the languages originated). Soon, archaeologists found supporting evidence: across eastern and southern Africa, pieces of pottery of similar yet diverse styles, alongside first-time traces of village life, metal-making, domesticated animals, and western African crops—all developed within the last 2,000 years. Northern sites usually dated back to earlier time periods than sites farther south. “You can clearly see a north-to-south progression,” says archaeological scientist Shadreck Chirikure of the University of Oxford. By the 1990s, however, some scholars began to question the idea that Bantu languages and skills spread through a migration. Couldn’t these cultural habits have spread while people stayed in the same place, with hunter gatherer communities passing along new technologies, languages, and habits to each other? Genetic techniques that could analyze people’s ancestry helped to resolve that debate. All of us share DNA with our relatives: 50 percent on average with siblings, 12.5 percent with cousins, 3.3 percent with second cousins, and so on. Though these proportions become minute the further back in time a shared common ancestor lived, statistical techniques can tease out patterns. Sure enough, a 2009 genetic analysis of 2,432 Africans found that Bantu-speaking people from western, central, and southern Africa are much more closely related than one might expect given their geographic distance. And in 2024, a team of scientists analyzed the genomes of 1,763 Africans and found the greatest genetic diversity among Bantu speakers in western Africa. As distance from the region increased, diversity declined. “This is a typical signature of a population expanding from one place to another,” explains coauthor Carina Schlebusch, a geneticist at Uppsala University in Sweden. But while linguistic and genetic data point to western Africa—particularly the Grassfields region of Cameroon—as the Bantu homeland, conclusive archaeological proof is thin. At the best-studied archaeological site in the Grassfields, a rock shelter called Shum Laka, pottery fragments appear dating from 7,000 to 6,000 years ago and become abundant by 5,000 to 4,000 years ago. Bowls and jars at first decorated simply with incisions and stamps and later bearing the marks of carved or braided tools rolled over wet clay are a possible, yet inconclusive, trace of Bantu ancestors. South of Cameroon, archaeologists have searched for artifacts in one of the Bantu-speaking people’s first destinations: the Congo rainforest. A handful of times between 2010 and 2023, archaeologist Alexandre Livingstone Smith of Belgium’s Royal Museum for Central Africa traveled via boat along the Congo River as part of a team led by linguist Birgit Ricquier. In a number of villages, Smith found pottery shards of a style consistent with that found farther west, dating back to 2,300 to 2,000 years ago. “That would be the potential first Bantu settlers of the forest,” Smith says. Many experts interpret the trails of pottery as evidence that ceramics traditions were brought by Bantu-speaking people all the way from their original home. What’s unclear, however, is when and how Bantu-speaking peoples acquired their farming and metalworking skills—and, in particular, whether they invented iron smelting themselves or learned it from neighboring, iron-extracting cultures. Scientists have been able to deduce the path that Bantu speakers took to eastern and southern Africa. Some scholars have argued that the path split into two before entering the Congo rainforest, with one group heading east while another went south through the forest. But evidence is now mounting that these groups crossed the rainforest together, and only upon reaching its southern fringes roughly 2,500 to 3,500 years ago did they split into east-moving and southward-moving groups. Some of this evidence comes from linguistic studies. Not unlike the evolution of species, languages diverge and transform over time, creating “trees” that linguists can reconstruct from similarities in grammar and vocabulary. In 2015, a group of researchers did this computationally using data from about 400 Bantu languages. They observed a much larger diversity—and deeper splits—among the Bantu languages spoken in the Congo rainforest, suggesting that Bantu-speaking ancestors spent a long time there, allowing their languages to diversify. Languages spoken farther east and farther south appeared to stem from a less diverse subset of this larger group, explains coauthor Koen Bostoen, a linguist at Belgium’s Ghent University. Later linguistic studies, as well as genetic analyses, have reached similar conclusions. In 2023, Beleza and her colleagues compared the genomes of Bantu speakers from Angola—south of the Congo rainforest—with those from the eastern African country of Mozambique and ones from Cameroon, the Bantu homeland. Remarkably, the eastern African samples were more genetically similar to those from Angola, hundreds of miles to the south of Cameroon and south of the rainforest, than to any of the samples from Cameroon. That points to a recent common ancestor in the Angola region—suggesting that the Bantu split into eastern and southern streams only once they reached the rainforest’s southern edge, Beleza explains. By the time the Bantu people commenced their movement east and south, they had already come into their own as a farming, iron-making, livestock-herding, and pottery-making people, Chirikure says: Many ancient Bantu settlements across eastern and southern Africa contain evidence of this Bantu “package.” Key to the Bantu’s success in southern and eastern Africa, experts suspect, was their sedentary lifestyle. This—and their iron tools—would have allowed them to produce more food and grow in number, propelling them to occupy new territories. Their lifestyle affected the landscape as forests turned into farmland, and it also affected the hunter-gatherer communities who were already living there. Some groups were likely crowded out and disappeared, experts suspect, while others were probably pushed to marginal regions, including Indigenous groups such as rainforest hunter-gatherers and Khoisan peoples, who continue to live in central and southern Africa, respectively, to this day. Still, scientists are learning that Bantu peoples had deep relationships with these two groups. In the 2024 genetic analysis, Schlebusch and her colleagues found that some Bantu-speaking groups in central Africa share about an eighth of their DNA with rainforest hunter-gatherers, and some southern African Bantu speakers share a similar amount with Khoisan groups. Clearly, their ancestors once intermixed. Curiously, in the case of the Khoisan, this mixing tended to arise from pairings of Bantu-speaking men with Khoisan women. One 2021 study of southern African Bantu speakers found that the DNA inside their mitochondria—which people inherit only from their mothers—had greater-than-expected proportions of Khoisan DNA. But the Y chromosomes, which come from fathers, had much higher proportions of DNA with Bantu ancestry. Some experts speculate that this pattern could be the result of Bantu men raiding Khoisan women or preferentially marrying Khoisan women for whom the traditional bride prices would have been cheaper. “Whenever there is a power difference… between two groups, it tends to melt down to this,” says coauthor Ananyo Choudhury, a geneticist at the University of Witwatersrand in South Africa. This pattern also left marks on the language. The characteristic clicks of Khoisan languages also occur in southern African Bantu languages Zulu and Khosa. Bostoen suspects that the children of Khoisan-Bantu pairings were, at first, bilingual. Eventually, “they lost their mothers’ languages, but they left a substrate of the mothers’ languages in the Bantu language.” Archaeologists are also studying the relationships between Bantu farmers and their neighbors. While there may have been conflicts over land, the groups also cooperated with and learned from one another, says Décio Muianga, an archaeologist at Eduardo Mondlane University and the research institute Kaleidoscopio in Maputo, Mozambique. He’s been excavating a rock shelter in Mozambique containing thousands of years’ worth of stone tools, wild animal bones, and wild plant remains—telltale signs of hunter-gatherers living at the site. But interestingly, Bantu-style pottery also appears. “My assumption is that the hunter-gatherers were getting these ceramics from the farmers,” Muianga says. “They were interacting, communicating, and exchanging resources.” There is evidence, too, for cultural exchanges: European anthropologists have reported that some Bantu-speaking groups would solicit San hunter-gatherers to conduct traditional rainmaking ceremonies, for example. Bantu social networks eventually extended well beyond Africa. By 1100 to 1600 AD, many of the complex state societies that Bantu speakers built developed trade networks across the Indian Ocean. Archaeological data shows they imported Chinese porcelain and glass beads; in return, they exported gold, ivory, and iron. In fact, a 12th century Muslim cartographer once wrote that iron from parts of southern Africa—likely referring to Great Zimbabwe’s iron-producing hinterlands, Mtetwa suspects—was deemed to be of much higher quality than that from other places like India. Mtetwa has found evidence that iron smelters near Great Zimbabwe would add bone fragments to the furnaces, perhaps as part of a smelting-blessing ritual, but one with scientific merit: This material contains calcium, which can accelerate the separation of iron from the rest of the ore. That may be a testament to the Great Zimbabweans’ iron-smelting skills: “Some materials introduced within ritual or medicinal practices may simultaneously have had technically beneficial effects on the smelt,” says Mtetwa. Though they’ve made great progress in reconstructing the Bantu world, scientists are aware of, and trying to address, the limitations of their research so far. Linguistic studies, for instance, have been a popular tool to reconstruct Bantu history, but Bostoen cautions that very few Bantu languages have been documented thoroughly enough to definitively trace linguistic trees back in time. Linguists may never get a complete dataset, because some old branches of the Bantu language family may have died out. Another limitation of computational Bantu language-family reconstructions is that they’ve usually assumed that languages have evolved in isolation after different Bantu groups separated. But there may have been extensive cross-pollination among languages as different groups met and interacted. In fact, when linguists factored in the possibility of contact among Bantu languages in a 2026 study, they couldn’t reconstruct the diversification patterns described by previous linguists, says coauthor Igor Yanovich of the University of Vienna, who wrote about the challenges of Bantu linguistic studies in the 2025 Annual Review of Linguistics. In his view, linguists should first focus on thoroughly documenting Bantu languages before attempting to use them to understand Bantu history. In hopes of adding new insights, Schlebusch is working to obtain DNA from ancient human remains. By revealing the identity of people who were living in particular places at particular times, this could help to elucidate when and where different Bantu communities moved into, overlapped with, or repopulated areas. “What we see more and more in genetic data is that the movement was probably complex—that it was these ‘spread over spread’ events,” Schlebusch says. Archaeologists, for their part, hope to fill in the vast gaps in the archaeological record of sub-Saharan Africa—by both conducting new excavations and re-examining existing artifact collections for fresh insights. But all this work requires funding for research and laboratories, especially from African governments, says Chirikure; he, like many of his colleagues, has largely relied on funding from Europe, which is limited. Without more funding, “you are not generating fresh ideas, you are not formulating new hypotheses and testing them using some of the cutting-edge science,” he says. Scientists from all three disciplines are hopeful that they will eventually tell the complete story of how an innovative people spread and diversified across sub-Saharan Africa, mixing traditions with those of earlier peoples and of others who still live there, to create myriad cultures, each one a unique blend of food, philosophy, language, music, and traditions. “If you travel from West Africa today all the way to East Africa, you have a unique cultural diversity that is very hard to find in many other parts of the world,” Muianga says. Combined with the legacy of other groups, he says, “this is the result of the Bantu migration.” This story originally appeared in Knowable Magazine. Knowable Magazine, 2026. British soldier ignored order to pull his SIM card, then got blackmailed
2026-11-07

British soldier ignored order to pull his SIM card, then got blackmailed

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
An Estonian newspaper recently revealed that a British soldier disobeyed a direct order to remove the SIM card from his cellphone before being deployed to a part of Estonia, approximately 90 miles west of the Russian border. As a result of his refusal to engage in this element of operational security (opsec), the soldier, Daniel Barton-Kennedy, had his phone hacked, and nude photos of himself were stolen and used as blackmail against him. Eventually, Barton-Kennedy was court-martialed for disobeying a lawful command, pled guilty, was reduced in rank, and ordered to serve 10 months in detention. According to a 21-page British appeals court document shared with Ars Technica by Eesti Ekspress, the Estonian newspaper that first reported on this saga, the story begins in January 2026. At the time, Barton-Kennedy was a corporal, with over seven years of service, serving in the 1st Battalion of the Duke of Lancaster Regiment of the British Army. His unit was deployed to participate in “Exercise Eastern Warrior” in Tapa, Estonia, roughly halfway between Tallinn, the capital city, and the Estonia-Russia border. While in the United Kingdom, on January 7, Barton-Kennedy, along with other members of his unit, were briefed on their orders and given specific instructions to “remove SIM cards and switch off location settings and to use a trusted virtual private network on all personal electronic devices.” Once the unit arrived in Estonia, days later, they were given further briefings and reminders of this policy. Despite this, Barton-Kennedy ignored the instruction. On January 19, Barton-Kennedy informed his platoon commander that he had received a message “from an unknown party who had acquired nude photographs of him from the photo section of his mobile phone and had identified him on social media.” Barton-Kennedy initially acquiesced to paying the attacker 100 British pounds ($133) but balked when he was asked to pay 250 British pounds ($333), and only then informed his platoon commander what had happened. Eventually, Barton-Kennedy was court-martialed and pled guilty at a hearing on July 30. During that process, according to the court filing, Barton-Kennedy understood the orders that were given to him at the time, but asked the court to “consider the personal circumstances” that he faced at the time. Without his phone, he told the court, he could not reach his daughter on her birthday, nor his pregnant partner. In a written statement, Barton-Kennedy said he was stunned when he received the first blackmail message. “Despite still being in a state of shock and uncertainty, I recognised that I had a duty to report the compromise without any further delay,” he wrote. Additionally, he said that he “deeply regrets” the incident and added that serving in the British Army “has been one of the greatest privileges of my life.” Barton-Kennedy appealed the sentence, which was denied in August 2026. In response to an inquiry for comment, Lt. Col. EP Mackie, a British Army spokesperson, told Ars in a statement that the security measures imposed are meant to protect the safety of military personnel. “Any breaches of Army security measures that might compromise personal safeguarding or operational security will be dealt with swiftly, and offending personnel can expect uncompromising disciplinary action,” he wrote.

Doctors pull knife from man's eye that went through his brain—and he's fine now
2026-11-06

Doctors pull knife from man's eye that went through his brain—and he's fine now

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-11-06

Visit Original Website
A man in his 20s showed up to a UK hospital with the hilt of a knife sticking out of his face. The 15 mm-wide blade hidden within went completely through his right eye and through his brain, with the tip of the blade hitting the top of his skull. Despite the ghastly injury, the man was alive and conscious when he arrived. He could still talk and move all four limbs. His doctors described him as being in a state of agitation. On the Glasgow Coma Scale, he scored a 12 (E3 V4 M5), indicating a “moderate” brain injury, just one point above “mild.” He could open his left eye when told, but otherwise kept it closed (E3 score); he could talk and answer questions, but was confused (V4); and he intentionally moved away when pressure was applied to his body (M5). In a report published Friday in BMJ Case Report, his doctors said they were concerned that in his confused, agitated state he may try to pull the knife out on his own. So, they swiftly sedated and intubated him. They put him on prophylactic treatments for bacterial infections, including tetanus. Then they used images to see what was going on in his head. The blade was entirely through the right eye socket and eyeball. It traversed the left frontal and temporal lobes of his brain, passing through in front of—and without apparently injuring—the corpus callosum, which connects the two sides of the brain and is full of nerve fibers. The blade also missed the left internal capsule, which holds another bundle of nerve fibers. In terms of damage to major blood vessels, doctors found that the blade was close to the left internal carotid artery, a major blood vessel, but didn’t seem to have damaged it. Doctors did find, however, that a branch of the middle cerebral artery looked to be indented by the knife. After consulting with various medical specialties, the man’s doctors moved to emergency neurosurgery. They opted to remove part of his skull to directly see into the brain, but used a conservative “pull-and-see” method to slide the blade out rather than invasively excavate it. After it was out, the doctors saw no signs of significant hemorrhaging. The brain wound was cleaned and drained before surgeons patched up his skull. His right eye, however, couldn’t be saved. Given its extensive damage, it was removed. The man then moved to intensive care, where he recovered rapidly. A day after surgery, doctors were able to remove his breathing tube. He developed some minor right-side weakness, but it resolved two days later. Scans of his brain showed steady improvement—a little bleeding and swelling, and there was some air in his brain tissue from where the knife had been. But otherwise, it appeared to be healing. He developed no infections and had no seizures. Despite the “dramatic intracranial trajectory,” his language function was preserved, and he had no loss of movement or coordination, they reported. He was discharged from the hospital and at follow-up appointments remained “neurologically well,” with ongoing eye and mental health care. As for how the injury happened, his doctors still don’t know. “The mechanism is unclear,” they wrote. However, they noted he had a history of depression. The doctors reflected on how lucky the man was to have survived and to have such a full recovery. Such injuries that go through the eye and the brain are rare. When they do occur, they’re usually from “high-velocity mechanisms” such as gunshots or other ballistic injuries. Knives, rods, and nails fit into the rarer “low-velocity mechanisms. “Such “low-velocity penetrating brain injuries carry substantial morbidity and mortality because of hemorrhage, vascular injury, abscess, meningitis, cerebrospinal fluid leak, seizures and neurological deficit,” they wrote. But such fortunate recoveries aren’t unheard of; they cited reports of other remarkably unexpected recoveries from seemingly devastating brain injuries. That includes the famous case of Phineas Gage, a railroad foreman who survived an 1848 accident that sent a large iron rod directly through his head. The rod entered below his left cheekbone and exited the top of his skull. (Although he survived and recovered, Gage’s personality was said to have changed after the accident. The doctors in the knife-wound case did not mention any such personality changes.) “Echoing classic and contemporary examples of severe frontal lobe penetration with unexpectedly preserved neurological function, including Phineas Gage and a reported nail-gun injury, this case highlights that outcome may depend less on external severity than on precise trajectory relative to critical vascular, cortical and subcortical structures,” the doctors concluded.

Ships loiter near subsea cables amid Iran's warnings to Trump
2026-11-06

Ships loiter near subsea cables amid Iran's warnings to Trump

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-11-06

Visit Original Website
Several tanker ships linked to Iran have been loitering over subsea Internet cables in the Gulf of Oman near the Strait of Hormuz—a possible indication that the Iranian government is preparing to attack such undersea infrastructure in response to any renewed US and Israeli strikes. The movements of three sanctioned tankers and an Iranian fishing boat have been tracked near major undersea cables by the maritime intelligence firm Windward in October, according to the Financial Times. Six active cables currently cross through the contested Strait of Hormuz, linking the Middle East to Europe, Africa and Asia, with two more cables having segments under construction in the region. Four countries, including Iraq, Kuwait, Bahrain and Qatar, depend entirely upon such subsea telecommunications connections running through the Strait of Hormuz chokepoint to link with the outside world. But other countries also depend heavily on the region’s undersea cable network—one third of India’s westbound Internet traffic goes through the Persian Gulf and Strait of Hormuz. One cable under construction, the 2Africa cable, is owned by a tech consortium that includes the US company Meta. That was one of the major cable corridors visited by the drifting Iran-linked ships. Windward used both ship-tracking data and satellite images to track the vessels as they slowed down and looped around near multiple cables, with some ships then “going dark” by switching off their automatic identification system (AIS) transponders that normally broadcast their location and other information. Such movement and behavioral patterns suggest the Iran-linked ships may be “mapping the cable routes, or placing charges or mines on them while dark,” Windward told the Financial Times. Windward highlighted the examples of two sanctioned tankers linked to Iran that spent days drifting “directly over regional cable corridors… both with no commercial reason to stop” in a blog post on October 26. Both ships carried no commercial cargo. Since the US and Israel first launched a major war with Iran in February 2026, Iran’s response beyond targeting US military bases, ships and aircraft has included attacking commercial shipping in the Strait of Hormuz and hitting oil refineries and other energy infrastructure along with airports in various Gulf countries. Iran has also made a point of threatening or attacking US Big Tech infrastructure in the Middle East, including launching multiple drone strikes on Amazon data centers. In May 2026, a spokesperson for Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps claimed Iran would “impose fees on Internet cables” belonging to US tech companies and other owners. But more recently, multiple Iranian officials and commanders of Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) have warned that they could directly attack the undersea cables running through the Strait of Hormuz. This came after President Donald Trump repeatedly threatened to renew major US military strikes on Iran and warned he could “annihilate” the country during a speech at the United Nations in New York on September 22. “Seven major cables run through the Strait of Hormuz, supporting global Internet operations and commercial data transmission,” one IRGC spokesperson said on October 20. “We remind the enemy of this.” The latest phase of the war has involved more limited US military strikes against Iranian targets, even as Trump has alternated between threats and touting the possibility of a diplomatic breakthrough. Meanwhile, Iran has increased drone strikes and other attacks on commercial ships to exert its own pressure on the United States, with October marking the highest number of ship attacks since the war began. Much damage has already been done. The limited fleet of cable-laying ships used for installation and repair has not been able to venture safely into the region since the war began, which forced the Meta-backed 2Africa cable to pause its planned expansion. The war has also compelled US tech companies and Gulf countries to accelerate efforts to develop alternative cable routes bypassing the Strait of Hormuz. The Middle East is not the only part of the world where threats to the Internet’s undersea backbone have intensified. Subsea infrastructure is increasingly at risk from sabotage and gray-zone warfare from the Baltic Sea to the Pacific Ocean.

Re-releases of Wing Commander, Ultima titles hit Steam and GOG
2026-11-06

Re-releases of Wing Commander, Ultima titles hit Steam and GOG

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-11-06

Visit Original Website
Many people are not big fans of game publisher EA right now for good reason, but for those who remember a better time in the 1990s, there’s a little good news. Twenty-five classic games from that EA era have been lightly remastered and updated, then placed for sale on Steam and GOG, including several Wing Commander games and nearly the entire Ultima series. Below is the full list of remastered or re-released games: In a lengthy blog post published this week, Jim Vessella, a senior producer at EA, described the efforts that went into remastering Wing Commander I-IV and Prophecy, Privateer 2, and Road Rash. It’s a long, detailed story that’s worth reading yourself, but here’s a top-level summary. After working on the mostly widely acclaimed Command & Conquer: Remastered Collection in 2019, Vessella writes that he learned about and visited a room at EA’s Redwood Shores campus that contained a large archive of poorly labeled or sorted physical media from ages past. In his free time, and with the help of others, he began cataloging this archive and discovered, among other things, that it contained virtually the entirety of the original masters of Wing Commander III and Wing Commander IV‘s full-motion-video cutscenes. The story that ensues involves building a small team of contractors, poring through the footage 17 hours a day on swing shifts to label and digitize it, and then coordinating with several key community members—particularly Matt Wells, who has made community enhancement patches for some of the Wing Commander games—to put together remasters of those games. The flagship offering of those remasters is much, much higher-quality full-motion video sequences than have ever been available before, but other enhancements include 60 frames-per-second gameplay, a plethora of newly available .INI tweaks, and full controller support (though looking at the Steam forums for the games, some folks are having trouble with said controller support). For Privateer 2—the little-remembered sequel to Wing Commander: Privateer—the crew couldn’t find the original, high-quality remasters of the FMV scenes, and ended up getting in touch with the game’s original cinematics director, Steve Hilliker, who had the tapes in his attic. Hilliker had always wanted to take a new color-grading pass, and he did just that, while also remastering and upscaling all of the cutscenes. Similar updates were given to Wing Commander Prophecy. The blog post also details GOG’s involvement in restoring and remastering Prophecy and Road Rash. For the latter, EA re-licensed the entire original soundtrack for re-release. The collection is on sale on GOG and Steam right now; previous owners of the old versions of the games on GOG and the EA storefront get these remasters for free. None of the games cost more than $5 each. One good story doesn’t undo a lot of bad news and questionable decisions from EA, but this shows that a few passionate people can do something remarkable for game preservation, even if some of the work was done on a volunteer basis outside normal working hours. (EA committed some resources to the restorations as the project progressed, though.) While the remasters aren’t perfect, the gaming industry could use news like this. It’s worth reading the full story in the blog post for a journey down memory lane and a look at how involved a restoration process like this can be.

Chainalysis says hackers stole credentials and tokens for all of its customers
2026-11-06

Chainalysis says hackers stole credentials and tokens for all of its customers

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-11-06

Visit Original Website
Blockchain analysis firm Chainalysis said that hackers have obtained sensitive personal information—including names, usernames, password hashes, and live tokens—for all of its users following a breach of its network security. The accomplishment could be a major coup for the attackers. Both Chainalysis and public sources have revealed a long list of high-profile customers, including government agencies such as the FBI, US Immigration and Customs Enforcement agency, and the US Air Force, as well as private companies such as Microsoft, Coinbase, and Barclays. It’s unclear if the data theft exposed customers to additional threats. Last Friday, Chainalysis said it had been the target of a cybersecurity incident in October that was similar to the hacks of other platforms during the same month. The external actor used AI agent-assisted offensive techniques to gain unauthorized access to Chainalysis systems and internal tools. An email the company sent to affected customers said some “non-sensitive customer data, such as user IDs, full names, email addresses, and organization role labels for users provisioned on our platform were accessed.” It’s not clear why the company considered such information “non-sensitive.” In a Wednesday update, the company said that part of the “ongoing multifront cyberattack” involved an “on-path attack” that exposed a subset of customer passwords, and that the attack vector had been identified and fixed. Also known as an adversary-in-the-middle attack, on-path attacks happen when a hacker gains access to a piece of infrastructure sending traffic back and forth between two parties. Chainalysis has yet to say where the infrastructure compromise occurred and if the data obtained from it was TLS encrypted. The company said later that day that an investigation two days earlier (on Monday) had revealed that “certain customer information including names, usernames, password hashes and live tokens for all Chainalysis users were exposed to a malicious actor.” It also said it had detected “abnormal activity” and, in response, took down its login page and know-your-transaction service, which monitors threats to cryptocurrency holdings in real time. The company has also removed access to its Reactor and Data Solutions services. In an update on Friday, Chainalysis said it had begun phasing in customer access to KYT and Address Screening and expected more customers to follow. It asked customers to submit their trusted IP addresses so they can be added to an allow list and to prepare to reset credentials once they regain access. The credential rotation is mandatory. The company says its investigation has yet to reveal any evidence that any investigation data has been accessed or Chainalysis attribution data has been manipulated. Several important questions remain unanswered. As noted, the source that made the on-path attack possible is still unknown. It’s not clear how useful the information obtained in such an attack would be unless the attackers also had the ability to decrypt the traffic they were monitoring. If that were the case, the attackers might have gained broad control of the company’s infrastructure. Chainalysis also didn’t explain why it waited until Wednesday to disclose the data theft of all customers’ personal information when its investigation discovered the exposure two days earlier. Further, the company has provided no details about the AI agents used in the attack and how they worked. A Chainalysis representative said the company wasn’t commenting beyond the statements in its blog post.
Fortune | FORTUNE
A CEO gave his employees Fridays off. Three years later, revenue is up about 160%
2026-11-08

A CEO gave his employees Fridays off. Three years later, revenue is up about 160%

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-08

Visit Original Website
In late 2022, Jake Kurtz, founder and CEO of Tampa-based social media agency Brick Media, wanted a better work-life balance. He had a wild idea: What if everyone worked just four days a week? After seeing European companies experiment with shorter workweeks on social media, he launched a 90-day pilot in January 2023, giving employees Fridays off without cutting their salaries. By April, he had made the schedule permanent. Nearly four years later, Brick’s revenue has grown dramatically. According to figures the company provided, revenue jumped 73% in 2023, another 39% in 2024, and 8% in 2025. Compounded, that’s roughly 160% growth in three years. Kurtz believes some of that success traces back to the four-day workweek, though he doesn’t claim the schedule alone explains it. He bets that giving employees a reason to stay makes the company more efficient and more competitive, even as workplace researchers say the four-day week has faded from the hybrid-work conversation and some leaders who tried it have dialed it back. At Brick, employees work four nine-hour days, Monday through Thursday. That’s a 36-hour week, four hours short of the traditional 40, with paid lunch breaks included. Employees are still considered full-time and eligible for benefits. That puts Brick between a compressed 40-hour week and the 32-hour model some researchers advocate. At Fortune‘s Workplace Innovation Summit in May, University of Georgia psychology professor Malissa Clark argued that the point of a four-day week is to cut eight hours, not cram 40 hours into four days. Brick’s schedule also contrasts with the long hours common in the related public relations field. Muck Rack’s State of PR 2026 report found that 55% of PR professionals surveyed had worked more than 40 hours the previous week. Social media doesn’t exactly take weekends off, but Kurtz said clients have been surprisingly understanding of the arrangement, and most content shoots, meetings and other work can be planned ahead of time. Employees still occasionally handle small tasks on Fridays or weekends, which he described as taking “five minutes here and there.” He estimated that more serious emergencies requiring several hours of work happen roughly once or twice a quarter. In those cases, nonexempt employees are eligible for overtime if they work more than 40 hours in a week, and the company brings in freelance contractors when needed. The rule he goes by: Don’t bury employees in work. Brick served 56 clients in all of 2022, compared with 85 in just the first half of 2026, according to Kurtz. A social media manager typically handles six or seven clients, with seven the upper limit. Once employees approach that threshold, Brick hires more people instead of piling more work on existing staff. That’s one reason revenue has grown so quickly. It’s also why profit margins have stayed roughly unchanged since 2022, Kurtz said, as payroll has risen alongside revenue. Employees also work from home most of the time. Only one of the four working days requires a trip to the office. For Kurtz, keeping employees happy is as much a business strategy as a cultural one. “If I can keep people here longer, and they like their job, and they’re communicating with similar people all the time, it just makes the business a lot more efficient,” he told Fortune. Experienced employees don’t need everything spelled out. Kurtz said colleagues who have worked together for years can sometimes resolve something in a two-minute hallway conversation that might otherwise take 30 minutes. He believes that efficiency translates into better client relationships and, ultimately, more business. Of the 20 new leads Brick received in October, 16 came from referrals, according to Kurtz. The company’s resignation numbers suggest employees are increasingly willing to stay. Brick recorded six voluntary departures in 2022, before the schedule change, compared with two in 2023, two in 2024, three in 2025, and none so far in 2026, according to figures provided to Fortune. Its workforce has grown from 15 employees at the end of 2022 to 25 today. Asked whether Brick could have achieved the same success with employees onsite five days a week and working frequent overtime, Kurtz said, “No, I really don’t… I think people would leave Brick Media for like a $5,000 raise.” He said he knows some employees have had better offers and chosen to stay. “People have a hard time leaving the four-day schedule once they experience it, because they start to realize that life is way more than just the salary,” he said. It’s the same case ThredUp CEO James Reinhart made on Clark’s panel at the Fortune summit, where he warned that companies sticking with five-day weeks will lose the competition for top talent. For Andrea Echeverry, Brick’s director of client experience, who has been with the company since before the schedule change, switching to four days hasn’t meant doing less work. “I think we have just found more ways to be more efficient with our time,” she told Fortune. She doesn’t mind squeezing the same workload into four longer days. In fact, she prefers it. Echeverry said “hustle culture has played a role,” explaining that people her age are still willing to work long hours but take a different approach to the traditional “work hard, play hard” mentality. She is a working mother whose husband also works full-time, and Fridays are now for spending time with her child and running personal errands. To Echeverry, it’s part of a generational shift in attitudes toward work. Rather than accept traditional workplace arrangements simply because previous generations did, she believes younger workers are more willing to question the norm. She isn’t alone in ranking balance over pay. In Randstad’s global Workmonitor 2026 survey, 46% of workers named work-life balance as their main reason for staying in their job, compared with 23% who cited competitive pay and benefits. “We’re sort of like redefining work-life balance,” she said. Kurtz doesn’t think every company needs to give employees Fridays off. Instead, he advises other CEOs to “think about what would make your employees want to work at your company.” “I just think that there’s a happy middle ground where you can still have a really good life and a really good business,” he said. “And if you can also extend that to your employees, then it’s not only a feel-good thing for the culture; it’s actually a business advantage that your competitors won’t have.” © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. Unrest in France looks like a ‘pretty big foreshadowing’ to economists nervously eyeing national debt at 126% of GDP
2026-11-08

Unrest in France looks like a ‘pretty big foreshadowing’ to economists nervously eyeing national debt at 126% of GDP

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-08

Visit Original Website
When U.S. markets watched as Paris metaphorically imploded just weeks ago, the consensus reaction was: “Let’s not do that.” Unrest ripped through the French capital after protesters initially highlighted failings in the public school system. The action snowballed into widespread expressions of dissatisfaction with the cost of living and job prospects. The macroeconomic health of the European nation is not in great shape: unemployment was 8.3% in Q2, real GDP growth was 0.4%, and wage growth was 1.7% at the national level. And underpinning it all is public debt, currently at 116% of GDP, with 30-year bonds topping 5.4% at the time of writing. And the U.S. bond market, flirting with a wrap on the wrist for its own policymakers presiding over mounting debt, stalled. In fact, 10- and 30-year Treasury yields even declined. “There’s a reason that the U.S. has pulled back a little bit in the last few weeks [and] why France has not,” Heather Long, chief economist at Navy Federal Credit Union, told a Bipartisan Policy Center (BPC) event last week. “It’s a couple of key indicators that differentiate us from France and suggest … that we still have a little bit of room to have these debates now, in the context of a K-shaped economy. “We’re at about 100% debt-to-GDP; they’re at 120% debt-to-GDP. They are further along that crisis curve, and they are growing at about 0.5%. We’re growing at just over 2%. That’s a pretty big difference. ” While economists largely agree that policymakers are perhaps naive in their hopes to simply grow the U.S. out of a debt crisis, starting from a point of strength rather than stagnation gives the economy a better chance of rebalancing its debt-to-GDP ratio. But Long added that France also differs from the U.S. in that the former has exhausted much of its revenue-generating capacity through taxes. Per the OECD, the “tax wedge” (the difference between labor costs to the employer and the corresponding net take-home pay of the employee) for French workers is 47.2%, according to its most recent data. The U.S., on the other hand, is down at 30%. “It’s certainly not easy to raise taxation in the U.S., but from a financial world perspective and the bond market perspective, there’s a lot more room to experiment and to grow in the United States than there is in France,” Long added. “To me, the biggest warning sign from France is really not just the K-shaped economy effect, but the generational effect that’s going on and to see young people really driving this latest protest … from the disparities that they’re seeing generationally, and that seems like a pretty big foreshadowing [of] what we could see here as we creep towards 120% debt to GDP.” As with any debt, the question of repayment isn’t if but when—and that’s the question budget experts are asking the public and politicians to think about. Phil Swagel, the director of the nonpartisan Congressional Budget Office, said policymakers have a series of choices to make: How they might prepare for growth to slow if spending is cut, which forms of tax to explore, and so on. “The other set of choices that you can think about with the deficit is about which generation is paying, so the longer we wait to make changes, that means some generations are … missing out on their share of the burden of the adjustment,” Swagel told the BPC event, “and then who within each generation will pay a tax.” He explained: “The longer we wait means some generations, in some sense, don’t share in the burden of the adjustment. Then, who within each generation is affected? I think there’s a societal consensus it will be progressive, not in the political sense, but in the sense that households with relatively high lifetime incomes will have more of a share of the adjustment. But that’s a societal preference, and there’s lots of room for disagreement in there.” Eleanor Pringle is an award-winning senior reporter at Fortune covering news, the economy, and personal finance. Eleanor previously worked as a business correspondent and news editor in regional news in the U.K. She completed her journalism training with the Press Association after earning a degree from the University of East Anglia. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. Fortune | FORTUNE
‘No vote, no warning, just plain math’: Social Security is six years from an automatic 22% cut—and most Americans have no idea
2026-11-08

‘No vote, no warning, just plain math’: Social Security is six years from an automatic 22% cut—and most Americans have no idea

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-08

Visit Original Website
More than one in five Americans don’t know that their Social Security checks could be cut by roughly 20% in about six years, according to a recent TIAA survey on Social Security. The program’s retirement trust fund, which pays benefits to retirees and survivors, is projected to run out of reserves in the fourth quarter of 2032, according to the 2026 Social Security Trustees Report. After that, incoming payroll taxes would cover only 78% of scheduled benefits. The resulting 22% cut would apply across the board, including to people already collecting. “Insolvency is a slightly misleading word for what the Trustees are projecting,” says Martin Mulyadi, YHB Endowed Chair in Accounting and Professor of Accounting at Shenandoah University. Payroll taxes keep flowing after 2032, he notes: “Social Security is not running out of money in 2032. It is running out of savings.” The stakes are large. Social Security pays monthly benefits to more than 71 million people, and it provides the majority of income for 43% of seniors, according to AARP. The Committee for a Responsible Federal Budget (CRFB) estimates that a 22% cut would cost a typical dual-income couple retiring in 2033 about $16,900 a year. CRFB has warned that the program is “on a collision course toward insolvency,” and Fortune has reported that its finances haven’t looked this bad since 1983. The alarm grew louder after the trustees confirmed the 22% cut in June. “America has a 25 trillion dollar problem,” says Michael Liner, a disability attorney and founder of Liner Legal, a law firm that specializes in Social Security disability benefits. “The gap between what Social Security has promised and what it can actually afford to pay.” The trustees now put that 75-year gap even higher, at $29.3 trillion in present value, up from $25.1 trillion in last year’s report. If nothing changes, Liner says, the cut arrives automatically: “No vote, no warning, just plain math.” For many retirees, he adds, it will be “the difference between getting by or not.” Social Security is funded mainly by payroll taxes. For decades, those taxes exceeded what the program paid out, and the surplus built up reserves in two trust funds: the Old-Age and Survivors Insurance (OASI) fund for retirees and survivors, and the Disability Insurance (DI) fund. That changed in 2021, when the retirement fund’s costs began exceeding its total income, according to the Congressional Research Service. Reserves have been covering the difference ever since. “In 2025, Social Security took in about $1.45 trillion and paid out about $1.61 trillion, and reserves covered the gap,” Mulyadi says. The disability fund is in better shape; the trustees don’t project it running dry at any point in the next 75 years. If Congress legally merged the two funds, combined reserves would last until 2034 and then cover 83% of benefits, according to CRFB. That would require a change in law, and it wouldn’t close the underlying shortfall. The public has noticed. In a June 2025 survey, just 36% of Americans told AARP they were confident in Social Security’s future, the lowest level since 2010. Much of the pressure is demographic. Social Security now serves roughly twice as many beneficiaries as it did in 1980, when about 35.5 million people collected. Lower birth rates mean fewer workers paying in, retirees are collecting for longer, and declining immigration has shrunk the pool of workers paying payroll taxes. Inequality plays a role, too. Payroll taxes apply only to earnings up to a cap, $184,500 this year, and high earners’ pay has grown faster than everyone else’s. The share of total wages subject to the tax fell from almost 87% in 1984 to roughly 83% today, according to Roosevelt Institute research reported by CBS News. “The Social Security trust fund is under strain because Congress has failed to update the program for the economy we actually have,” said Elizabeth Wilkins, CEO of the Roosevelt Institute, a progressive think tank. “Too much income now flows to the top, where it escapes Social Security taxation.” The last time Social Security came this close, Congress acted. The 1983 amendments gradually raised the full retirement age from 65 to 67 and, for the first time, subjected some benefits to federal income tax. “We’ve had two instances in our history where it was suggested Social Security was going to go insolvent,” says Ernie Wingard, an advisor at Capital Choice Financial Group, “and in both of those instances, at the last minute, Congress was able to come to an agreement that saved it.” That 1983 fix was designed to keep the program solvent for 75 years. If the retirement fund runs dry in 2032 as projected, it will have lasted about 50. Lawmakers have plenty of levers. Options modeled in the American Academy of Actuaries’ Social Security Challenge tool and in Social Security Administration analyses include: “Any fix has to close that gap through some mix of more revenue, such as a higher payroll tax rate or a higher cap on taxable earnings, and slower benefit growth, such as a later full retirement age or changes to the benefit formula,” Mulyadi says. “Each option has trade-offs, and the longer Congress waits, the larger each adjustment has to be.” The fight is over the mix. In a debate covered by Fortune, two economists who disagreed on whether to phase out Social Security still agreed that wealthy Americans would have to pay more. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, meanwhile, argued in June that economic growth can shore up the program without benefit cuts. “This is absolutely something that can be fixed,” says Nancy LeaMond, AARP’s executive vice president and chief advocacy and engagement officer. “Congress has hundreds of tools in its toolbox to strengthen Social Security and fill that gap of 20 percent or so without cutting the payments that Americans have earned for a lifetime of hard work.” “This should be a wake-up call: Congress needs to act,” AARP CEO Myechia Minter-Jordan said in a June statement. “Americans have worked hard and paid into Social Security their entire lives, and they deserve to count on it when they retire.” Whatever lawmakers choose, they have about six years to choose it. For households, Mulyadi recommends planning with a range rather than a single number. “Log in to your my Social Security account at ssa.gov, look at your projected benefit, and test your retirement budget at about 75 to 80% of it,” he says. “If the plan still works, a cut is an inconvenience,” he says. “If it doesn’t, you still have time to save more, work a little longer, or rethink when to claim.” © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. So much ‘liquid gold’ could hit the market that the world’s top producer is withholding output—but supplies can’t stay ‘extra virgin’ for long
2026-11-08

So much ‘liquid gold’ could hit the market that the world’s top producer is withholding output—but supplies can’t stay ‘extra virgin’ for long

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-08

Visit Original Website
A bumper harvest of olives threatens to jolt the olive oil market, prompting the world’s top producer to suppress supplies for the first time ever. On Friday, Spain’s agriculture ministry ordered farmers to hold back 12% of their production until the end of September 2027, requiring them to store supplies on their farms or at third-party facilities. “The measure aims to ensure farmers’ profitability and also to protect the interests of olive oil buyers,” it said, according to the Financial Times, adding that “greater stability to the market” will benefit producers and consumers. While the move is unprecedented, the European Union allows olive oil supplies to be held back, unless it leads to excessive market distortion. The current harvest season for so-called “liquid gold” began in September, and favorable weather this past spring promises to reverse years of drought and heat that kept olive production low. This season, Spanish olive oil production is projected to jump 23% to 1.6 millions tons, marking the fourth-largest harvest ever. The agriculture ministry seeks to keep 188,000 tons off the market. But expectations for more supply have already sent olive oil prices down. At mills in Andalusia, they averaged 2.93 euros per kilogram last weekend, down about 15% in about two months. The olive oil industry has pushed back against the government’s attempt to prop up prices, pointing out the market is global while the order only applies to Spanish producers. Kyle Holland, senior analyst at commodity data firm Expana, also noted that olive oil degrades over time, telling that FT that after several months in storage ”very quickly that olive oil is not going to be extra virgin anymore.” He added that top olive oil buyers already have hefty supplies, so ordering farmers to store away some production would have little immediate impact on prices. Spain isn’t the only country dealing with wild swings in key commodities. The Quebec Maple Syrup Producers, which oversees Canada’s strategic syrup reserve, recently warned the stockpile will be completely empty by February. While new supplies will continue to be produced, the reserve won’t be able to help ease any wobbles in output, which depends heavily on the weather and can be volatile. Demand has increased in recent years as producers have diversified to more market beyond the U.S., but last year saw an especially big spike after President Donald Trump launched his global trade war. To get ahead of anticipated tariffs, Americans rushed to buy up Canadian syrup supplies. As a result, total syrup exports jumped nearly 16% last year, up from 9% growth in 2024. Meanwhile, the Iran war has forced oil-consuming countries around the world to drain crude stockpiles. In the U.S., the Strategic Petroleum Reserve has fallen to the lowest level since 1982. Elsewhere, Japan has a rice reserve that had to be drawn down last year as prices soared amid bad weather and looming threats from natural disasters that sparked panic buying. But this year, Japan has already swung to a record surplus. And China tapped its strategic pork reserve in 2019 after an outbreak of swine fever sent prices higher. But this year, prices have slumped as the anemic consumer economy has weighed on demand while the government’s prior encouragement to boost production has now resulted in oversupply. Pork prices have plunged 39% over the last four years. To help prop up the market, China has repeatedly purchased pork for the strategic reserve. Jason Ma is the weekend editor at Fortune, where he covers markets, the economy, finance, and housing. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Alabama Power spent more than $6 million on ads last year — and customers can be billed for half of them until 2029
2026-11-07

Alabama Power spent more than $6 million on ads last year — and customers can be billed for half of them until 2029

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
This article originally appeared on Inside Climate News, a nonprofit, non-partisan news organization that covers climate, energy and the environment. Sign up for their newsletter here. On a busy election day, the Alabama Public Service Commission requested that Alabama Power voluntarily stop billing customers, effective immediately, for television commercials and other ads it buys to promote various aspects of its business. Alabama Power is the largest electricity provider in Alabama, with territory covering about two-thirds of the state. Though it is a monopoly, the company spends millions each year on advertising, touting its reliability, customer service and other aspects of its business. In recent months, the company has launched a series of television commercials touting a rate freeze it announced late last year amid heavy criticism over rising electricity prices. A state law passed earlier this year will prohibit Alabama Power from including the costs of those commercials in customers’ electric bills, meaning the powerful utility will have to find all of the dollars in corporate profits. But the new law won’t take effect until 2029. Currently, Alabama Power is allowed to bill customers for half of its non-public safety advertising costs. During Tuesday’s meeting, PSC President Cynthia Lee Almond moved to “request” that Alabama Power voluntarily begin excluding those costs immediately. “I don’t see any reason why we shouldn’t go ahead and take steps now to begin that process, rather than waiting until Jan. 1, 2029,” Almond said during the meeting. Almond then made her motion, which was not on the meeting agenda, but came after the regular report from the commission’s electricity division. “I move that the commission request that Alabama Power consent to exclude all advertising expenses from retail rate setting until after Dec. 31, 2028, when the new law takes effect,” Almond said. “Assuming Alabama Power consents, this interim measure would be reflected in its [Rate Stabilization and Equalization] filings for 2027 and 2028.” Commissioners Jeremy Oden and Chris Beeker IV voted yes after a brief discussion. According to the company filings with the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission, Alabama Power spent more than $6 million in general advertising last year, roughly $4 per customer. It’s unclear from the federal filing how much of that total was passed on to customers or how much might be impacted by the new prohibition. Alabama Power did not immediately respond to a request for comment. Daniel Tait, executive director of Energy Alabama, said the move was a boon to Alabama Power customers, who faced some of the highest electric bills in the country in recent years. “We are grateful for the leadership of President Almond and the full commission for acting to end customers paying for Alabama Power’s advertising,” Tait said. “Once in place, Alabama will have one of the strongest customer protections in the nation on this front.” This will be among the last actions of the commission under its current construction before a major overhaul takes effect. In January, the commission will expand from three members to seven. Outgoing Gov. Kay Ivey has appointed four new commissioners, who will take office in January, including the PSC’s two first-ever Black members. Oden and Beeker will also leave the commission in January after being defeated in Republican primaries. Cullman County Sheriff Matt Gentry will take Oden’s Place 1 seat, and former state auditor and PSC Commissioner Jim Zeigler will replace Beeker in Place 2. Zeigler, a perennial candidate in Alabama across several decades, will return to the commission 48 years after leaving the PSC. He served one term from 1975-1979, during an era of skyrocketing energy costs and protracted political battles over electric rates between Alabama Power and then-Gov. George Wallace. Almond will be the sole remaining member of the current commission. The new seven-member commission will be overseen by the state’s first secretary of energy, who will be selected by Gov.-elect Tommy Tuberville. The new secretary will set the commission agenda, and overruling the secretary will require a vote of five of seven commissioners. Tuberville has not yet announced his pick for secretary of energy. The only statutory requirement is that the secretary “must have experience in utility regulation or energy policy or a related field.” © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. In-N-Out’s billionaire owner vows iconic chain ‘will always be’ in California and says leaving the state would be the ‘dumbest business decision ever’

In-N-Out’s billionaire owner vows iconic chain ‘will always be’ in California and says leaving the state would be the ‘dumbest business decision ever’

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
Lynsi Snyder, president and owner of In-N-Out, moved her family to Tennessee recently but reaffirmed that the iconic burger chain will never abandon California. In an interview with actor Terry Crews that In-N-Out posted online, she was asked how the company will retain its California DNA as it expands to more states around the country. “This is where we started. It’s where we are now, and it’s where we will always be,” Snyder replied. Her grandparents opened the first In-N-Out in Southern California in 1948, and the company has gradually expanded to other parts of the state—and to other parts of the country. While far from being the biggest chain, it retains a cult following that has helped its expansion as people who moved out of California make pilgrimages when restaurants open in new states. But Snyder, who grew up in Northern California, revealed last year that she and her family were moving to Tennessee, saying that raising kids was difficult in the state and that “doing business is not easy” there either. That sparked fears In-N-Out might leave California, prompting the burger heiress—who is worth an estimated $7.3 billion—to clarify that her departure from the state doesn’t mean the company will too. In a video posted to her Instagram page last year, Snyder told followers: “We’re not leaving California, or leaving our roots behind. Each one of our locations is here to stay.” Still, there has been a corporate exodus from California to states like Texas, where Elon Musk has relocated the headquarters for Tesla and SpaceX. In-N-Out has also clashed with state authorities, such as when it refused to comply with officials and request that customers must show proof of vaccination to make purchases during the pandemic. And the company reluctantly raised prices after California imposed higher minimum wages for fast-food workers in 2024. But during her latest interview, Snyder addressed the fears about In-N-Out, which maintains headquarters in California and Tennessee. “There were rumors about ‘they’re gonna close all their California stores.’ I’m like what where did this even come from? That’s unimaginable. I can’t even imagine that — dumbest business decision ever,” she said. Snyder added that In-N-Out continues to grow in California, with “lots of stores” slotted for the other cities in the state. At the same time, the company has expanded to Nevada, Arizona, Utah, Texas, Oregon, Colorado, Idaho, Washington and Tennessee. Snyder suggested that Mississippi, Arkansas, and Oklahoma are under consideration, with New Mexico also in the works. But she insisted the company is taking a slow and steady approach to growth that won’t compromise on the quality of its food her grandparents’ values, adding that In-N-Out won’t be able to accommodate every request to open a new location. As for her own family’s relocation to Tennessee, Snyder described it as “kind of a God thing” and said she originally wanted to move to Colorado because she loves the mountains. But her husband floated the idea of going to Tennessee instead. While they already had made plans, she recalled that “something just kind of clicked.” “I felt it and then started looking at it, just praying, and I’m like ‘I think you’re right. I think that’s where we’re supposed to go,'” Snyder said. Jason Ma is the weekend editor at Fortune, where he covers markets, the economy, finance, and housing. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. Silicon Republic
‘The more sophisticated our tech becomes, the more human we need to be’
2026-11-06

‘The more sophisticated our tech becomes, the more human we need to be’

Silicon Republic | 2026-11-06

Visit Original Website
Gina London discusses how AI training and skill development should not be viewed as competing investments. In the AI age, it can be tempting to ‘go all in’, adopting artificial intelligence and automating workplace tasks. Certainly, it brings a medley of benefits, from less time spent on mundane tasks to increased productivity in labour-intensive industries. But at what point do we start to neglect the skills that came before, such as critical thinking, communication and research? “I recently coached a senior executive who had become so reliant on AI to write his presentations that his team barely recognised his voice anymore,” said Gina London, a CEO, leadership strategist, author and former CNN anchor. She explained that while his presentations were both polished and beautifully structured, his colleagues “weren’t connecting with him”. She said: “Communication isn’t simply the transfer of accurate information. It’s the experience we create for another person. Think about an engineer explaining a technical risk to a board, or a scientist communicating a breakthrough to colleagues outside their discipline. “AI can organise the information, but the human still needs to recognise confusion, invite questions and establish trust,” she explained. “Empathy, curiosity and active listening aren’t optional extras. They’re what help turn technological progress into meaningful human outcomes. “The more sophisticated our technology becomes, the more deliberately human we need to be,” she said. But it isn’t a case of adopting an ‘all or nothing’ mentality, according to London. When asked why human reasoning can never be replaced by automation, London is quick to note that we have to be careful when using the word ‘never’. “AI is already performing forms of reasoning that would have seemed extraordinary just a few years ago. But intelligence and humanity aren’t interchangeable,” she said. “AI may become increasingly sophisticated, but it doesn’t possess a human soul. Reasoning at work isn’t just about arriving at a technically sound answer. It’s about understanding consequences, weighing competing priorities and taking responsibility for decisions. “I encourage leaders to distinguish between generating an answer and taking ownership of a decision. AI can contribute enormously to the first. We shouldn’t surrender the second.” And what of the consequences for organisations that fail to adequately balance AI and human-centric skills? For London, this risks creating organisations that may be extraordinarily efficient at producing information, but increasingly ineffective at communicating it. She explained: “When every email, performance review and leadership announcement sounds as though it came from the same chatbot, people begin to wonder whether anyone has genuinely considered what they’re saying. “There’s also the risk of weakening our own capabilities. If we routinely outsource explaining, questioning and persuading, we’re getting less practice at doing those things ourselves. In technical environments, where collaboration across disciplines is essential, that’s particularly dangerous. “An excellent engineer who cannot explain a risk to a non-technical colleague may struggle to secure support. A brilliant researcher whose manager never listens may eventually stop offering ideas. The cost isn’t simply poorer communication. “It’s missed innovation, weaker relationships and decisions that fail to benefit from the intelligence already in the room.” Part of the solution, finds London, is identifying how skills, both soft and technical, complement one another – rather than simply existing side by side. “Stop treating AI training and human skills development as competing investments. They should reinforce each other. If you’re teaching employees to use AI for reports, presentations or project updates, teach them to evaluate, challenge and communicate those outputs too.” Employers should also work to protect genuine conversation and identify the elements of the workplace that genuinely require automation and those that don’t. London said: “Sometimes the most valuable moment in a meeting is an unexpected question that changes everyone’s thinking. And leaders must model these behaviours themselves. People need to see their managers listening, questioning and communicating with intention.” Moments that “carry significant human consequences” are a good place to start, she finds, for example when delivering difficult feedback, supporting someone through a personal crisis, addressing conflict, ethics-based decision making, or moments that require accountability. “AI may help a manager prepare for those conversations, but it shouldn’t become a convenient way to avoid having them,” she said. “There’s also a distinction between automating administration and automating relationships. “An AI-generated meeting summary might save everyone 20 minutes. Wonderful. But an automated message telling someone their role is being eliminated is another matter entirely. In technical organisations, I’d also protect the human exchanges where colleagues challenge one another’s assumptions. “A laboratory discussion or engineering design review can be valuable precisely because someone notices something unexpected. And let’s not forget the informal conversations that build workplace culture. Not everything valuable at work can be measured as a productivity gain.” When it comes down to it, London is of the opinion that there is a real opportunity to use automation as a means of becoming better prepared, not less personally engaged. “AI can help us produce more,” she said. “The challenge is making sure we don’t lose the ability to mean more to one another.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Technology developed by the space sector has applications across adjacent industries, including in areas such as defence, communications and environmental research. Ireland also established its first ESA Phi-Lab at the Irish Manufacturing Research facility in Mullingar. NovaUCD-based climate-tech Proveye announced a partnership last year to bring its AI-driven, satellite-based precision fertiliser technology to more than 1,000 dairy and beef farmers. Celtonn, also based at the institute, was awarded a contract to develop a 130GHz amplifier for next-generation 6G-capable satellite systems. The company was also selected for the European space-tech business accelerator, Cassini. Dublin-based maker of advanced acoustic materials Lios bagged €6.25m in funding from the European Innovation Council accelerator to scale its technology. Meanwhile, in 2026, Ubotica – which develops an AI-powered satellite platform used in maritime security and climate change detection – raised $11m in June. The European Commission has confirmed the adoption of the 2027 European Innovation Council (EIC) Work Programme, which will unlock funding of €1.8bn intended to support Europe’s deep-tech entrepreneurs and researchers. This will be the final EIC funding package to be implemented under the Horizon Europe 2021 to 2027 research strategy. It aims to support research breakthroughs as they become market-ready innovations, as well as strengthen Europe’s ability to develop and scale strategic tech. As part of the funding, there will be additional support given to women-led start-ups via a redesigned Women TechEU programme, from where grants will now be doubled to €150,000 per start-up. The initiative will also see the introduction of a new European Prize for Women Innovators in Quantum. This will be a recognition similar to the current European Prize for Women Innovators, according to the Commission. The EIC has a budget of more than €10bn for the 2021 to 2027 period, under Horizon Europe. Since its inception, according to the Commission, it has supported more than 800 companies and nearly 700 research projects, providing funding across the full innovation life cycle. Cosmic “Loophole” Could Let Black Holes Split In Two Like Dividing Cells
2026-11-07

Cosmic “Loophole” Could Let Black Holes Split In Two Like Dividing Cells

Futurism | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
Black holes can potentially divide and multiply like cells. This scenario is unlikely but theoretically possible, new research suggests, if a black hole spins up fast enough — the maximum rate physically possible, in fact. Then the black hole “can wear pants,” funneling itself down two separate tubes of spacetime to come out as separate bodies, they report in a new study published in the journal Physical Review D. “In our study, we investigate which physical laws normally prevent a black hole from splitting into two,” coauthor Francesco Sannino, of the University of Naples Federico II in Italy, said in a statement about the work. “We also investigate if certain circumstances may allow a split to happen.” You’ve probably heard of black hole mergers, but a black hole split is pretty esoteric territory. Central to the work is entropy, a concept in thermodynamics that describes the degree of disorder in a system. The universe naturally tends toward disorder; it’s why things decay. Or why your house gets messier over time — unless, crucially, you introduce extra energy into the system by tidying it up . If a black hole were to divide when it’s not rotating, it would require a decrease in entropy, or in other words, a decrease in the disorder in the system, which makes the scenario “thermodynamically unfavorable.” That’s what pushed the researchers to explore what happens when a black hole rotates at a high speed. Intriguingly, they found a “loophole” that causes the black hole’s outer boundary, the infamous event horizon, to form a pants-like shape with two “legs” across space and time, with one resulting black hole coming out at each end. But adding an additional constraint to this scenario, the original black hole needs to be spinning at the maximum possible speed. Any slower, and its progeny wouldn’t separate enough to prevent them from merging into one again. “The two resulting black holes must also be able to separate rather than immediately fall back together,” Sannino said in the statement. “When we include this requirement, the window largely closes again, except possibly for black holes rotating close to their maximum possible rate.” Though the work demonstrates it’s possible, “we cannot yet give a single probability for a black hole to split,” Sannino said. “It depends on how gravity behaves beyond Einstein’s theory, and we are working to quantify this.” It may be possible, though, to detect a split through gamma-ray telescopes. But in case you were imagining giant cosmic monsters spawning more underlings, the researchers’ work suggests this split is more likely to happen with small black holes.
