Scientists Study Difference Between Men Who Pee Standing Up and Sitting Down
2026-11-07

Scientists Study Difference Between Men Who Pee Standing Up and Sitting Down

Futurism | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
One key debate still swirling around men’s health: is it better to pee standing, or sitting down? It may seem like a silly question, but there is some scientific merit to the question. Previous studies have found that men with prostate issues or urinary complications could be better off sitting, pointing to the possibility that plopping down may help loosen up pelvic floor muscles. Unfortunately, the evidence available is far from conclusive — which is probably what inspired the Colombian Urology Research Group to plow ahead with a new study published in the journal Neurourology and Urodynamics. The research, first flagged by Science Alert, took a look at the “posture adopted during voiding” of 52 male volunteers, 24 of whom stood while the rest sat. Each group went on a 24 hour “washout” break in between tests. During the examination, researchers looked at maximum urine flow rate, as well as the total volume of urine expelled in one go. Despite previous polarizing studies on the topic, this piece of research yielded a dull conclusion: it just doesn’t seem to matter. “In healthy men, voiding posture did not significantly modify the maximum urinary flow rate or voided volume,” the team wrote. The one difference between the two postures, the scientists noted, was in what might be called “acceleration.” “The sitting position was associated with a shorter time to maximum flow,” the scientists wrote, noting that it’s a “small difference that is unlikely to be clinically meaningful and was partly dependent on a single outlier.” More on research studies: Alarming Study Finds That Most People Just Do What ChatGPT Tells Them, Even If It’s Totally Wrong

Richard Norton’s Death Linked to Black Market Peptides
2026-11-07

Richard Norton’s Death Linked to Black Market Peptides

Futurism | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
Famed actor and martial artist Richard Norton died last year after taking unapproved peptides, according to a newly released coroner’s report. Norton, who was known for his villainous roles in Hong Kong action films including 1997’s “Mr Nice Guy,” and more recently the latest “Mad Max” films, died on March 30, 2025, at the age of 75. At the time, his cause of death wasn’t known to the public. Now the coroner’s writeup, as reported by NBC News, reveals that he died at his home of sudden cardiac arrest “following self-administration of recently commenced peptide therapy” that he was undergoing with the supervision of his doctor. Norton, however, had been exceeding the dose that he was prescribed. Just two days before he died, he told his nurse that he’d been taking “double the prescribed dose” of two peptides called CJC1295 and Ipamorelin. The nurse then “advised Richard” that “he ought to only administer the prescribed dose.” The reason Norton was taking peptides was probably to help keep up his athleticism in his old age. He told his doctor that he was interested in peptide therapy, saying he had already taken the drugs under another doctor’s guidance for “fatigue, reduced physical recovery, shoulder pain and loss of lean muscle mass.” The peptides he was taking are commonly blended together to stimulate growth hormone production and help with recovery. Australia’s ABC News reported that his death is believed to be the first in the country that’s known to be caused by unapproved peptide use. Norton’s downturn came suddenly. He took blood tests before being approved to start peptide therapy less than two weeks before he died. For the next week, he reported feeling fine and that the treatment was working. Just a few days after checking in with the nurse, though, Norton complained that he felt “hot and flushed and then collapsed and became unresponsive.” Paramedics arrived, performed CPR after observing he was experiencing cardiac arrest, and took him to the hospital. He died two days later. Peptides are short chains of amino acids that are typically injected to bring about purported medical benefits. Some have made it through clinical trials and can be prescribed legally, like Ozempic and Zepbound. But synthetically produced peptides that haven’t been well studied have simultaneously achieved explosive popularity in biohacking spheres and among wealthy figures and celebrities — and while many swear by their benefits, the long term health consequences remain hazy, as does their legality. Many are produced in China and sold by dubious vendors. The coroner didn’t mince words on unapproved peptides, noting that they’re not approved by Australia’s health regulators or “any global regulatory authority for human use,” with their use “mostly considered experimental in humans,” and their “toxicology is largely unknown.” More on peptides: RFK Jr Opens the Floodgates on Experimental Peptides, Alarming Scientists

Remember When Bosses Were Forcing Their Workers to Use AI? They’re Now Realizing They Made a Huge Mistake
2026-11-07

Remember When Bosses Were Forcing Their Workers to Use AI? They’re Now Realizing They Made a Huge Mistake

Futurism | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
For years, many bosses have been adamant that employees use AI at all costs — despite research suggesting there’s little to gain from it in terms of productivity, evidence that flies in the face of the tech industry’s repeated promises of a workplace revolution brought on by the tech. But now that execs are drowning in meaningless “workslop,” they’re starting to sing a very different tune. As Business Insider reports, one CEO of a tech recruiting company suddenly found herself being inundated with Slack messages from employees that they’d clearly generated using AI, leading her to implement a new policy that strictly forbids the practice for internal communications. She’s not the first. One anonymous CEO got so fed up, they threatened to “fire the next person” who blasted them with unedited ChatGPT slop, as Inc reported earlier this year. Instead of fundamentally reshaping white collar industries for the better, it’s finally dawning on bosses that the extensive and often counterproductive use of AI tools could be doing far more harm than good. Case in point, in a new study published in the journal The Leadership Quarterly, a team of researchers led by Hebrew University of Jerusalem PhD student Samuel Meyers found that employees who doubled down on using tools like ChatGPT are being trusted less by their employers, actively hurting their chances of promotion. That’s even at workplaces where the use of AI tools are still actively being encouraged, suggesting there’s a paradoxical price workers who embrace the tech are paying. In a series of experiments, the team asked supervisors to decide whether to trust an employee’s report, while knowing whether they had used AI or not — but not basing their judgment on the quality of the work itself. Employees who didn’t use AI had a 68 percent chance of earning their trust. When they used AI, that number shrank down to just 24 percent. “AI is changing not only how we work, but also how we judge the people we work with,” said coauthor and Hebrew University of Jerusalem senior lecturer Yochanan Bigman in a statement. “An employee may use AI to produce better work more efficiently yet still lose their manager’s trust simply because AI was involved.” As a result, “organizations need to recognize that adopting AI is not just a technological challenge — it is also a psychological and managerial one,” Bigman added. Beyond eroding trust, executives have also found that the use of AI agents and coding tools in particular have sent costs soaring, seriously hurting their companies’ bottom lines and often forcing them to reverse course and cut spending. Meanwhile, researchers have yet to detect a meaningful windfall from using AI at work, making it an expensive distraction. Polls have shown that the vast majority of CEOs haven’t seen any productivity gains. It’s a particularly unfortunate reality as the job market continues to deteriorate, with or without any AI-related productivity boost. Employees are now stuck between a rock in a hard place: either follow their supervisors’ instructions and use AI, or earn their trust by ignoring those calls. Meyers and his colleagues worry this growing tension could give rise to “shadow AI” — undisclosed use of the tech at work, that could further hurt their career prospects. More on work and AI: There Are Now Growing Signs That AI Is Hurting Employment in the Job Market

OpenAI Hears Voters’ Overwhelming Rebuke To Data Centers, Vows to Build More of Them

The “blue tsunami” that swept over the midterm elections made it clear that voters are sick of tech billionaires meddling in politics, opposed to data center construction, and deeply suspicious of the AI industry. In an interview with CNBC on Friday, she vowed that OpenAI would keep pouring money into data center construction so it can please its corporate customers — voters be damned. “When I think about the midterms, I need to keep thinking about how do we keep investing to meet that customer demand,” Friar told CNBC’s Sara Eisen in the interview. “It starts early with having built relationships with all of the hyperscalers, and it will continue to mean that we raise capital.” While Eisen is focused on “customer demand,” she should probably start paying attention to what voters are asking for. But all that is moot to OpenAI’s Friar — since, in her eyes, people want AI. “The demand for AI is real,” Friar said in the interview. “I don’t think anyone’s arguing with that, or if they are, I think it’s a losing argument.” In all, she doesn’t believe that the election drastically changes anything for the company. To her, it seems, the backlash to AI is more or less a messaging problem. “If the midterm means more focus on data centers and communities, we’re going to keep doing the work to get into those communities and to build trust with them,” Friar said. She put it in more transactional terms elsewhere in the interview: “We are going to keep building out those relationships with communities so we can get the compute we need.” If Friar’s intentions are to portray the company as unphased, it’s coming a little too late. In the wake of the elections, OpenAI moved swiftly to announce “new commitments to the communities” where it’s building data centers, which included a promise to no longer use non-disclosure agreements while discussing data center construction with government officials. After the midterms, things are looking rocky for Elon Musk and data centers
2026-11-07

After the midterms, things are looking rocky for Elon Musk and data centers

TechCrunch | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE. Democrats’ historic victory in this week’s midterm elections could also have a dramatic effect on the tech industry. In dozens of local ballot measures, voters rejected data centers. And even when they weren’t voting on the issue directly, they were electing politicians — like Texas’ new governor-elect Gina Hinojosa — who campaigned on the promise of restricting data centers. On the latest episode of TechCrunch’s Equity podcast, Kirsten Korosec, Sean O’Kane, and I discussed what the midterms mean for the tech industry. Sean kicked it off by noting that Elon Musk “seems to have basically nothing out of donating half a billion dollars” to Republicans, while his companies are now headquartered “in a state that has, remarkably, turned about as blue as it could possibly turn.” And Kirsten said that while data centers probably weren’t the main cause of the “blue wave,” they’ve become an “an increasingly grassroots issue, in part because of how hyperscalers and companies have gone about” getting their projects approved. And now, “we’re already seeing companies like OpenAI all of a sudden go, ‘Actually, we can change our policies.’” Keep reading for a preview of our conversation, edited for length and clarity. Sean O’Kane: First off, let’s talk about the fact that one of the biggest data center builders was also the largest donor to the Republican Party. Elon Musk seems to have gotten basically nothing out of donating half a billion dollars — maybe even more, by the time we see the final count — and in fact is now sitting there with all of his businesses in a state that has, remarkably, turned about as blue as it could possibly turn for the first time in a very long time, if ever. That, I think, is the biggest thing hanging above a lot of this discussion. But, there were also a bunch of ballot measures up around the country that would have paved the way for data centers to get built faster. Almost all of them got shot down, or voted against. [Actually, many of the ballot measures would have imposed restrictions or moratoriums on data centers, and almost all of those measures passed.] With [Democrats] in power, there is a consensus view, at least in the tech world, that this is going to mean we have a lot of roadblocks ahead for data centers, especially in places like Texas, where [you have] folks like the governor-elect [Gina] Hinojosa, who has spoken pretty openly about this being a campaign promise and something that she wants to focus on once she takes power, now that she’s beaten Greg Abbott. It’s the thing that I think the tech world is maybe a little too focused on. I’m curious what you guys think about that. Anthony Ha: I don’t necessarily think that they’re too focused on it, but I understand why it would feel a little bit disproportionate — the data center ballot measures, certainly there were a number of them, but it wasn’t something that was on the ballot everywhere. And in different races, the dynamic might have been a bit different, but there was this undercurrent and sense that it seems to be an issue that reaches across the country, that is bipartisan in a lot of ways, but that the Democrats seem to have been more effective at harnessing that energy. And of course, there’s all kinds of reasons why the Democrats did so well on Tuesday, but it does seem like anti-AI and especially anti-data center sentiment was a big part of it. That seems like a pretty potent political force. The next two years, [and] potentially beyond that, are going to be more difficult for tech companies in general, and hyperscalers, data center companies in particular. I also imagine that they have to be a little bit worried about: Is this going to continue to be an issue, not just with the people who got elected this year, but potentially in 2028 and beyond? Kirsten Korosec: Absolutely. I don’t think that the data center issue is what brought everyone to the polls and led to the quote-unquote “blue wave.” However, I will say that data centers have been an increasingly grassroots issue, in part because of how hyperscalers and companies have gone about getting these data centers [approved] — very secretive, oftentimes requiring NDAs of a elected officials or appointed officials, not sharing information about the scale of a project in very environmentally sensitive areas, including in the Southwest, where there’s very much a water scarcity issue. So I think that a lot of folks — Republicans, independents, and Democrats —  all saw this as wrong. They were motivated by it, but I don’t think it was a singular issue. Now, here’s the interesting thing. There was a lot of waving of hands about how [AI companies] can’t possibly operate under any other circumstances. And we’re already seeing companies like OpenAI all of a sudden go, “Actually, we can change our policies.” They just released what they call a compute campus policy blueprint. And guess what? They’re not going to require NDAs anymore. They’re going to share their plans and supposedly operate in less secrecy. I think what this shows is that when you put up either a regulatory certainty in the marketplace, or at least signals of going in that direction, then corporations will respond. If you don’t do that, they will not necessarily do the thing that we hope that they will do. Anthony: One thing I want to emphasize is that they’re calling it this [their] “compute campus policy.” And part of what that’s doing, beyond laying out these policy changes, is also an attempt to rebrand data centers, because they have become such a toxic phrase. So they’re not data centers anymore, they are compute campuses, and I’m curious, whether either of you think that’ll take. Sean: I mean, this is ridiculous. Come on. It’s not going to change anything. It’s going to cause them more effort. For a company that likes to say that it’s no longer focused on side quests, OpenAI still loves to go in different directions a lot. And this is just the latest kind of thing where it’s just like: Why are you spending time on this at all? It doesn’t really seem like it’s going to move the needle. Kirsten: I have a great idea for OpenAI. Because in the spirit of making threatening things seem cute, they should 100% create a compute campuses avatar, [a] cutesy thing, and then just present that instead of a power-, energy-sucking, water-sucking data center. Anthony: And if these are just lovely compute campuses, they should just build their own offices there or work next door, see how that works out for them. Something else that I’m wondering about is, to what extent the election is maybe the second part of a one-two punch. [We’ve just seen] a big shift in how Wall Street is reacting to a lot of these hyperscalers and AI companies and this sense that [investors] are not excited about the idea that they should be spending all this money building new data centers, spending all this money on compute. And so there’s a financial incentive not to build all these data centers. I’m very curious how that’s going to play out. [From a regulatory standpoint,] it’s probably going to be harder to build data centers. Wall Street is probably not going to reward you for building data centers. But at the same time, to get to the scale that they want to get to, they are probably going to need to build data centers somewhere. A memo circulated earlier this year claimed that technology executive Alice Huang was recruited by China’s Ministry of State Security to spy on Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, according to multiple media reports. The New York Times, which had previously said Nvidia’s chief executive had been the target of Chinese intelligence, was the first to report on the memo, which was reportedly based on U.S. intelligence. The Financial Times subsequently reported that the memo also claimed Jensen Huang had a romantic relationship with Alice Huang, an executive at a Singapore-based company called Megaspeed. An Nvidia spokesperson told the NYT that Jensen Huang’s interactions with Alice Huang were “not unusual,” and that the company does not share classified information with its customers. A separate NYT report examined New Matrix, a Chinese company authorized by the U.S. Commerce Department to buy advanced Nvidia chips. But at the office address listed by New Matrix, reporters only found “a hollow gray shell,” raising concerns that it could be a front for a blacklisted private equity firm. Parents want safer phones for kids. These companies are answering the call.
2026-11-07

Parents want safer phones for kids. These companies are answering the call.

TechCrunch | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE. As parents become increasingly concerned about the potential dangers of unrestricted smartphone access for children, a growing number of companies are developing devices specifically designed for kids. Today’s kid-friendly phones typically feature touchscreens, cameras, and communication capabilities similar to regular smartphones. However, the software on these devices is heavily modified to remove or restrict access to web browsers, social media, app stores, and other distractions. Parents can usually manage these devices through a companion app, allowing them to approve contacts, monitor locations, set screen time limits, select available apps, and, in some cases, receive alerts regarding cyberbullying, explicit content, or online predators. In addition to these modified devices, some companies offer a more minimalist approach, creating devices that focus solely on calling and texting. There are also home phones designed specifically for children, like Tin Can and the recently launched Pinwheel Home. Here’s a look at the major players shaping the kid-phone market. The Bark Phone is probably one of the most popular kid-specific smartphones on the market. Built on Samsung Galaxy hardware and powered by Bark’s monitoring software, it only allows calls and texts with approved contacts. Bark continuously scans texts, emails, photos, and supported apps for signs of cyberbullying, grooming, suicidal ideation, sexual content, and other potential concerns, then sends alerts to parents. A big standout feature is that parents can gradually unlock web browsing, apps, and other features as their child matures. The phone also includes GPS tracking and screen-time management. Current pricing for the standard model is $240, plus a required wireless plan beginning at $29/month. Higher-tier plans add internet access and unlimited texting. Gabb is a well-known name in the kid-friendly phone category. Unlike Bark, however, Gabb focuses less on monitoring content and more on eliminating online risks altogether. Its phones don’t include social media, a browser, or an app store. Instead, children get calling, texting, and a carefully curated selection of preloaded apps, including a camera, calendar, and calculator. The company also touts its “worry-free” music streaming library with millions of songs. Additionally, parents can view location and manage certain device settings, while optional services such as “Gabb Guard” help block spam calls and unwanted texts. Current phone pricing starts around $159.99, with cellular service starting around $24.99 per month. With Pinwheel, parents approve every app, control contacts, schedule screen time, create custom routines, and monitor location history. One standout feature is the ability to create different “modes” throughout the day. For example, limiting the phone to calling and navigation during school hours before unlocking additional features after homework. Pinwheel phones start around $119, while the Caregiver Portal subscription begins at $14.99 per month without cell service. (Cellular service can be purchased separately through participating carriers.) The company recently launched two landline phones for $68 and $79. It also offers a smartwatch at $160. Launched in 2022, Teracube offers a tailored version of Android known as Thrive OS. This enables parents to approve every app download, filter web browsing, set app-specific and overall screen-time limits, monitor location, and create customized routines for school or bedtime. However, because it has more traditional smartphone features, it’s often recommended for older children. Thrive currently sells for $99, with plans starting at $35 per month. Unlike the other products in this roundup, Ooma MyPhone isn’t a mobile phone at all. This kid-focused home phone is designed as a modern replacement for the traditional household landline. The service allows calls only between approved contacts through its Trusted Circle feature, offers scheduled Quiet Hours, provides address-based 911 service, sends emergency alerts when 911 is dialed, and lets parents review call logs through an online portal. There are no apps, internet browsing, texting, or social media. The phone costs $99.99, while service starts at $7.99 per month. For $100, Tin Can resembles a landline phone with a distinctive tin can design, which is the inspiration behind its name. This device does not require a traditional phone jack, but connects via Wi-Fi. Only approved contacts can make calls, effectively preventing spam or unauthorized communications. Parents can easily manage contacts through a user-friendly companion app. Tin Can offers a free plan for calling other Tin Can users, as well as a $9.99 per month plan that allows calls to all approved contacts. A newer entrant in the space, Waffle recently opened preorders for its screen-free devices: Waffle Home ($119), a landline phone, and Waffle Go ($169), which looks like a portable walkie-talkie or kids’ toy with three simple buttons that can only make voice calls and listen to voice notes. Parents can approve contacts via the companion app, as well as see location and control when devices can call. TextNow, the Canadian telecommunications company, recently launched its new mobile phone for kids. It’s called Yello! and while it looks like a normal touchscreen smartphone, it doesn’t have an app store, social media, or unrestricted web access. Parents can see their kids’ location and battery life, as well as control things like the games they play and who they have in their contacts. Yello! costs $149.99 upfront, with a $19.99 per month plan. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence. Binance’s chief financial officer Kaiser Ng is leaving the cryptocurrency exchange. According to Bloomberg, co-CEO Yi He said in a statement that Ng’s departure will allow him to “pursue a new venture after more than five years at Binance,” and that a transition plan has been “implemented over the last half year.” The announcement comes barely more than a week after hackers stole $6.3 billion worth of cryptocurrency from Binance users. The company froze withdrawals for several days; after accounts were unfrozen, users withdrew a net $3.6 billion in the first few hours. Binance and the FBI blamed a North Korean group called TraderTraitor for the hack. The company also said that it would cover all losses, promising, “Binance will bear the financial impact of the stolen funds. These losses will not be passed on to users.” Ng will be replaced as CFO by Justin Yek. Amazon’s new basic Kindle proves you don’t need all the bells and whistles
2026-11-07

Amazon’s new basic Kindle proves you don’t need all the bells and whistles

TechCrunch | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE. In October, Amazon announced its refreshed Kindle lineup, its first significant design overhaul in years. The highlights included a new edge-to-edge design that eliminated traditional raised bezels, thinner and lighter Paperwhite and Colorsoft models with better displays, and page-turning accessories like a Bluetooth remote and a magnetic cover with page-turn buttons. However, given the impact of the global memory-chip shortage driven by the AI boom, the new Kindles also debuted with pricing that had increased by $40 to $50 across the lineup. That makes it more difficult for the average consumer to consider buying one of the more premium devices. For that reason, the Kindle I most wanted to get my hands on was the 6-inch entry-level device, which starts at $169.99 for the ad-free version. With the price hikes, this is the Kindle that will remain the most affordable to new e-reader buyers. The question I weighed was whether or not the basic Kindle is even worth buying at this price when there are arguably better Kindles on the market. The answer, after a couple of weeks of testing, is yes — if you can live without the bells and whistles and prefer a smaller, lighter device. On the 6-inch Kindle, there’s no warm light to reduce eye strain, no color display, no auto-adjusting brightness, no option to use the page-turn cover, and it’s not waterproof. Obviously, because of its size, the screen is smaller, as is the battery. Battery life is 6 weeks compared with the Paperwhite’s 12 weeks or Colorsoft’s 8 weeks. Still, even this basic Kindle has seen improvements of its own, mostly from a physical design perspective. The plastic 2026 Kindle is 11% lighter and 14% thinner than the 2024 model, weighing 140g versus 158g and measuring 6.85mm thick versus 8mm. While this seems like a small change, the 18-gram difference is noticeable and particularly welcome for a device you’re likely to carry in your purse or bag. Plus, it makes it easier to hold for hours on end when you’re really binging your way through a book. (The aluminum version, meanwhile, is slightly heavier at 162g, or 4 grams more than the 2024 Kindle, though it still benefits from the slimmer design.) And despite being the “starter” Kindle, the 6-inch basic device still received the new flush-front screen update. This makes it more comfortable to hold in your hand, especially for those long reading sessions. Amazon explained that you don’t have to worry about no longer having a bezel to rest your thumb upon, either, as the new Kindles have been updated to detect a dead zone in the capacitive touch that will readjust to account for the fact that your finger is there. In practice, that doesn’t mean you can just press your thumb down anywhere on the Kindle’s screen as you hold it — doing so would register this as an attempt to highlight or look up a word. Instead, it means you can rest your thumb or fingers on the outer edge of the Kindle’s screen (the colored border) without triggering an unintentional tap, even though some of your finger will be touching part of the Kindle’s capacitive screen. In terms of the software, the new basic Kindle is a bit snappier than its 2024 predecessor. This is most noticeable when opening books, which Amazon claims to be 30% faster. If you’re coming from the 2019 Kindle Basic, the 2026 version is an even bigger upgrade, as you gain USB-C, more storage (from 4GB to 8GB to now 16GB or 32GB), the new flush screen, and the screen resolution jumps from 167 ppi to 300 ppi. It’s faster, thinner, and lighter, too, and the battery lasts weeks longer. Another perk about the basic Kindle beyond its small size (this thing fits in your jeans’ back pocket!), is that you can now pay a little more for an upgraded version with an aluminum backing instead of plastic and more storage. The new $189.99 aluminum option comes in Seagrass and Alpine Sky hues, and ships with 32GB of storage. Meanwhile, the $169.99 plastic model offers 16GB of storage. However, even if you go for the less expensive option, you can at least now pick a fun color other than black (aka Graphite), as Amazon has introduced a cheery purple option called Ube. (I almost wish I’d gone for this one, just for the whimsy of it, even though I prefer the feel of the aluminum.) I should point out that you can use the Kindle Click, the new Bluetooth remote for page turning, with the basic Kindle, but I’m not sure why you’d need to, if not for some accessibility-related reason. The 6-inch Kindle was already the smallest, and now it’s lighter and easier to hold, too. (But perhaps I just don’t get the appeal of the remote? I’ve barely used the remote with my Kobo either!) The big thing to consider here is whether you want to spend this much on a Kindle that lacks a warm light. While this is something of a personal preference, for some readers, no warm light is a dealbreaker. The warm light shifts the color tone of the Kindle’s display from a cooler bluish-white to a softer amber glow, which is meant to help reduce eye strain and improve reading conditions in low light or at night. Whether you need this feature depends on where you like to read and how long your sessions tend to be. If you read nightly in bed with little light, you might prefer paying extra for a $199.99 16GB Kindle Paperwhite. (That version only comes in Graphite. The 32GB Paperwhite can come in Seaglass, too, but is $249.99). If you often read by the pool or on vacation, you also might want to upgrade to a waterproof Kindle. But if you can live without the extra features and want the best purse- or pocket-sized device, the 6-inch Kindle is still a viable option. Sequoia shutting down its Scouts program marks the end of an era
2026-11-07

Sequoia shutting down its Scouts program marks the end of an era

TechCrunch | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE. Sequoia this week announced a broad overhaul in a memo posted to X, a move that includes closing its London office and shutting down its scout program. Observers warn that it would be a mistake to read too much into the London closure, since the British capital has a thriving AI scene. On the other hand, the decision to end scouts says more about where venture investing is headed. That’s partly because 17 years ago, Sequoia pioneered the scouts model, in which select founders and operators write checks on the firm’s behalf. Amid increased competition for deal flow, many VC firms followed suit. But a consensus now seems to be emerging that the model has reached its limits in its current form. Not everyone was sold on the model in the first place. “They pioneered the scout program but [to be honest] it never made sense to me!” VC Sheel Mohnot wrote on X this week. “The scouts themselves don’t really care that much about the investments, you can still miss (like they did with Uber), not sure what it gets you. I suspect other firms will shutter theirs too,” he added. To be clear, Sequoia didn’t make a case against scouts in the memo. Instead, it framed its decision as part of a broader need for more focus. But its goal of “putting more energy behind fewer people and things” is likely to resonate with others who’ve questioned the ‘spray and pray’ approach to scouting. Rival firm Andreessen Horowitz (a16z), for instance, rolled out a global scout network aimed at spotting opportunities in the U.S. and also in smaller European hubs. But a16z partner Gabriel Vasquez told TechCrunch earlier this year that the firm had revamped its program to double down “on the best people.” Under this new approach, Vasquez explained, scouts get more resources but are also expected to be more active, such as by co-hosting dinners with the firm. “They get more budget, but we have reduced the number,” he said. This also seems to indicate that it is VC firms that have been driving an overhaul of the model. For founders, being a scout is attractive: it brings them closer to firms they may want checks from someday, or even a partner role with, and being seen as someone who can write a check can raise their standing among other founders. Some scouts will undoubtedly mourn the end of Sequoia’s program. Among them is Station F boss Roxanne Varza, who wrote on LinkedIn that she was “sad” to hear that the program was getting cut off, and “hope[d] that other funds [w]on’t follow suit.” Varza, a former Sequoia scout from 2020 to 2025, told TechCrunch that she had no information beyond what has been shared publicly, but observed that becoming a scout had often been a stepping stone for aspiring investment managers. “I cannot name all of the incredible scouts that have gone on to join or build funds, but there are quite a few. And the scout programs may have helped them get there — allowed them to build an investment track record, allowed them to build relationships with various funds directly, and more,” she wrote to TechCrunch. According to Varza, the program’s impact went beyond the individual level, instead helping the entire ecosystem become more mature. “I believe they have helped to foster the investment culture and early-stage investment landscape that we now see in Europe, for example,” Varza said. While others may agree with Varza about the role these programs have played, they may still have run their course for two reasons. First, seasoned operators may get more financial upside as angels. When it’s their own money, they get to keep the returns, too. Second, leading VC firms are now big and well-known enough that the best founders tend to come to them, so they worry less about missing deals, except at the very earliest stages or in niches and geographies where they have less reach. Still, the pull of those early and niche deals is why scouts programs are much more likely to get revamped than to disappear entirely. “While Sequoia may be cutting the program, venture partner-style deals are likely to continue,” said Varza in her email to TechCrunch. “Scouts was just a way to scale the format. I don’t imagine Sequoia would end all forms of venture partners, but they are full of surprises.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Europe’s Bomb Threats Raise Fears of Hybrid Warfare
2026-11-07

Europe’s Bomb Threats Raise Fears of Hybrid Warfare

TIME | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Air traffic at Milan International Airport resumed after security checks by bomb disposal experts on two aircraft that had been the subject of attack threats, in Ferno, Italy, on Nov. 5, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A wave of bomb threats this week targeted dozens of flights across Europe before expanding to include newsrooms, leaving authorities scrambling to find those responsible. The bomb threats—all since identified as hoaxes—came through almost daily via anonymous calls and emails beginning Nov. 2. On Monday, a Wizz Air flight traveling from London to Bulgaria was diverted after a bomb threat was received mid-flight, making an emergency landing in Budapest. At least seven passenger flights were targeted the following day, which led to emergency landings, searches, and diversions. More than two dozen additional flights were targeted Thursday, and threats continued into Friday. Affected flights connected countries including Italy, Ireland, Spain, Bulgaria, Romania, Poland, Finland, Austria, Denmark, Belgium, Portugal, France, Greece, Serbia, Czechia, and the United Kingdom. An infographic created based on available information as of Nov. 5, 2026. Omar Zaghloul—Anadolu via Getty Images Authorities, including the U.K. Transport Secretary, have since described them as “hoaxes,” as no attacks were carried out and each affected flight was landed safely. But the hoaxes were not harmless; they affected thousands of people’s flight plans, struck fear into potential travelers, and impacted the carefully structured aviation ecosystem. The malicious efforts expanded Friday when a warning was sent by email to several European broadcasters claiming that bombs had been planted in their buildings, forcing evacuations at outlets like Sky News, Bulgaria’s Nova TV, and Irish public broadcaster RTÉ. “What happened this week is just a small part of what awaits Europe in the near future,” the message sent to broadcasters said, according to Reuters. European Union Transport Commissioner Apostolos Tzitzikostas strongly condemned the threats on X on Friday, saying that he has activated EU-level aviation security information-sharing mechanisms. He added that it was “too early” to draw conclusions on whether the hoaxes were linked. Ireland’s government, meanwhile, said Friday that “it seems likely that this is a co-ordinated campaign of disruption.” Experts tell TIME that the threats themselves might be the point—launching a fear campaign meant to disrupt systems and put millions of people on edge. Spanish Foreign Minister José Manuel Albares told Spanish broadcast news on Friday that the threats were an “attempt at hybrid aggression and psychological warfare.” Elisabeth Braw, a senior fellow at the Atlantic Council, which is a nonpartisan think tank for global affairs based in D.C., says that these kinds of threats fall into what she calls “gray zone conflicts,” meaning that they’re “in the gray zone between war and peace.” For example, Italian Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni told an Italian news station on Friday that “what’s being attacked is civilian infrastructure,” rather than military or weapons infrastructure. This puts European nations in a tight spot, Braw says, especially if the actor is another nation. “If you attack another country's military, that country's military will strike back. That is the nature of war,” she explains. “Whereas if you target civilian infrastructure, what is that country going to do? It’s not going to respond with military means, simply because then you would have war.” Read more: What Does the Iran War Mean for the Threat of Attacks in the U.S.? Here’s What Experts Say Bomb threats against airliners are not new, Braw says, but their scale and frequency this week stand out. “What is concerning this time is it's such a significant number, and the fact that these have come in such close succession, and it comes against the background of all these other attacks and really geopolitically motivated crimes targeting European countries,” Braw adds, referencing another destabilization operation that recently hit Europe. Cyberattacks targeted refrigeration systems at grocery stores in at least nine countries across Europe and at blood banks in France, following similar attacks on French supermarkets in mid-October. Two pro-Russian hacktivist groups claimed responsibility for the cyberattack campaign on supermarkets Wednesday, though their involvement remains unconfirmed. U.S. federal agencies released a fact sheet the following day that called on hospitals, transportation systems, and food retailers to “defend against this activity by using strong authentication.” Kremlin Spokesperson Dmitry Peskov distanced the Russian government from the cyberattacks on Thursday, calling any allegations “absurd” and “unfounded.” Robert Jordan, a program director at the Royal United Services Institute, tells TIME that it might be “tempting” to ascribe the threats to hostile states like Russia. “Certainly there are well-founded instances of Russian and Iranian groups recruiting people online using secure communications applications to conduct similar activity,” he says. At the same time, he continues, there is no way yet to discount that this is an individual or non-state-affiliated group exploiting safety standards in the region to “cause chaos.” Before the parties responsible can be ascertained, or their motives investigated, security personnel must identify where these threats are originating from, he says. Until they do, disruptions to airports are likely to continue, Jordan says. Even suspected hoaxes demand serious consideration, Braw adds, given the potentially devastating consequences of a real attack. “You have to take every threat seriously in the sector, but especially in aviation, where the consequences would be so utterly devastating if an incident were successful, and so that's why we have seen all these airliners making emergency landings,” she says. “All of that causes disruption and it causes anxiety among passengers, and it also causes significant financial costs and losses for the airlines.” French prosecutors are investigating whether foreign powers are connected to the bomb threats, Agence France-Presse (AFP) reported Saturday. The purpose of the investigation is to find “the authors of the emails who disseminated this false information ... and to determine any potential links they may have to foreign powers,” the Paris prosecutor’s office is quoted as saying. Some of the emails were signed "fighters of the Male State," which matches the name of a radical misogynist group in Russia, though there has been no confirmed link, according to AFP. The group's founder, Vladislav Pozdniakov, denied any involvement, saying on social media Friday that the "movement does not exist since 2021." “Identifying who is doing this will rapidly be escalating up the priority ladder for law enforcement and security services across Europe,” Jordan says. “In the meantime, it is likely that airlines and airports will be meeting to determine how much extra screening scrutiny the system can bear.” Even after learning who made the bomb threats, European nations might not be any closer to knowing how to respond to similar hybrid attacks in the future, Braw says. “As a law-abiding democracy, you can't say, ‘Well, if they threaten our airlines, we will threaten them.’ That’s not how democracies operate,” Braw says. “Yet you can't allow these sorts of activities to continue to proliferate, and that is a dilemma for European governments every single day these days.” The dilemma is becoming more and more “acute,” she adds, as the threats accelerate and grow more sophisticated.

The Silver Linings Of an Aging World
2026-11-07

The Silver Linings Of an Aging World

TIME | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The world is undergoing one of the biggest demographic transitions it has ever experienced. About two-thirds of humanity now lives in countries where fertility is too low for each generation to replace itself. The result is rapidly aging workforces in most of the world, and the population in parts of Europe is already shrinking. One way to measure aging is by looking at the old-age dependency ratio, a demographic indicator that captures the number of people in working age (15 to 64) relative to the number of elderly, retired people (64 and older). By 2035, for instance, Italy’s old-age dependency ratio will have risen from an already high 39% to 50%. Greece, Portugal, and Spain are close behind. A similar demographic shift is underway in Latin America, including in Cuba, Puerto Rico, Costa Rica, and Colombia. Just a few decades ago, these countries had much smaller elderly populations compared to Europe and thus had lower old-age dependency ratios. Now they are catching up with Europe on this measure. In some parts of Asia, demographic concerns are more heightened as populations are expected to age even faster than in Europe and Latin America, especially in China, South Korea, Taiwan, and Thailand. By 2035, the old-age dependency ratio is expected to increase from 21% to almost 34% in China. In Hong Kong, it is expected to exceed 55%. North America’s population will continue to grow and will age more moderately, mostly because of immigration. In the United States, the old-age dependency ratio is forecast to increase from around 28.8% today to about 34% by 2035. Immigration policy will shape the exact extent of population change and aging in the country. The implications of this global trend are far-reaching, and for many, simply alarming. As the International Monetary Fund (IMF) puts it starkly: “For those countries where fewer workers are available and labor force participation rates drop, economic output is bound to fall.” Other forecasts warning about pressures on living standards, fiscal problems, and broader social challenges are equally gloomy. And aging will affect politics and social dynamics in various, often unforeseen ways, opening up new fissures in society. Yet demographic change can also create  opportunities, especially in societies whose institutions can adapt and that can make the right technological investments. These are epochal challenges. We cannot afford complacency, but alarmism is not warranted either. Several of the consequences of aging are not as dire as many assume and claim. Aging shifts the balance of electoral power toward older generations, which complicates the politics of pensions. The stakes are especially high in Italy, Portugal, and Spain, where public pension obligations are ballooning and are expected to exceed 15% of GDP by 2045. An aging population could also make it harder for a society to form a consensus on forward-looking investments that may take decades to pay off, such as those necessary for a more complete green transition. Some researchers have pointed to the age divide in the Brexit vote in the United Kingdom. In the 2016 referendum on whether Britain should leave the European Union, most British voters 65 and older chose Leave, while those under 25 overwhelmingly voted Remain. With stagnant or declining populations in Europe and parts of Asia, and a growing young population in Africa, the pressure for international migration will intensify as well. Recent experience suggests that migration could become an even greater source of political tensions. These are valid concerns, but the glass may be more than half full. Aging is accompanied by longer, healthier lives. The prospect of more healthy years extends the horizon of all age groups in society and could become a basis for new coalitions supporting public investments for the present and the future. Younger and expanding populations are no recipe for consensual politics either. I worked with the economists Leopoldo Fergusson and Simon Johnson to study what happened in developing countries in the early decades after World War II as their mortality rates declined sharply. Antibiotics and other new medicines, along with public health campaigns against diseases such as malaria, helped more people survive. More children lived past infancy; populations grew rapidly. We found that these population expansions and growing numbers of younger people in the developing world led to greater domestic conflict and a much higher likelihood of civil war. Competition and conflict over resources were fierce. In many newly independent countries with weak institutions, these conflicts turned into long-lasting civil wars that inflicted a grim toll on society and the economy. Ecuador, El Salvador, Guatemala, Peru, and the Philippines all experienced massive increases in their populations between 1940 and 1980, with populations tripling in some cases. Internal conflict became increasingly common. The conflict was exacerbated further when population increases coincided with an abundance of natural resources, as in oil-rich Nigeria, which experienced roughly 150% growth in population between 1940 and 1980. When larger populations compete for the same resources, existing fault lines in a society are exacerbated. In many of these countries in the 1950s and 1960s, investment and resources did not keep up with the growing labor force. As my research has documented, the expected economic dividend from better health and a larger population did not materialize, and most of these countries experienced a slowdown in their per capita income growth. That observation offers a way to think about the complexities that aging and slower population growth bring to the economy. Many commentators, just like the IMF, are convinced that aging and stagnant populations will create a huge economic burden, but the evidence from the past 70 years suggests a more nuanced and more optimistic outlook. When populations surged in several developing nations in the 1950s and 1960s, their GDP did not keep up, and their per capita incomes fell compared to countries with stable populations. Over the last 70 years, where birth rates fell, mostly in the industrialized countries, GDP did not suffer, and per capita incomes grew more rapidly. Economic responses were varied. In my research with the economist Pascual Restrepo, we found that countries undergoing faster aging invested more in technologies such as automated machinery, which ameliorated worker shortages and boosted productivity. Several industries including automobiles, chemical manufacturing, and machine-tool production, once heavily reliant on able-bodied prime-age workers, have been at the forefront of installing robots and other new technologies, especially in countries experiencing rapid aging. It is partly for this reason that economies with the greatest labor scarcities––Germany, Japan, and South Korea–– significantly increased their exports in these once labor-intensive industries during the 2000s and 2010s. My recent work with economists David Autor, Keelan Beirne, and Andrew Scott shows that because of such investments in technology and shifts in the composition of the economy, lower birth rates over the last 70 years have not been associated with slower economic growth. Overall GDP was largely unaffected by falling birth rates, and as a result, lower birth rates translated into significantly higher income per capita. A similar pattern appears across labor markets in the United States. Areas where birth rates fell further have experienced similar growth in aggregate earnings and consequently much higher wage growth for workers. Rising (per capita) incomes can also facilitate new compromises in politics. Sluggish real wage growth has been one of the reasons why people throughout the industrialized world have turned to anti-establishment parties. A robust economy that needs labor and offers workers new, well-paid jobs could reverse this dynamic. None of this is reason for complacency. The economic research I have cited so far shows that higher productivity was the result of technological investments and other adjustments. Unless such investments continue as populations age and population growth slows further, there is no guarantee that the past will be a reliable guide for the future. Countries also need institutional strength. A major challenge is the growing strain that demographic change places on political and economic institutions. New technologies often take hold through new firms, but those firms may struggle to gain a foothold in several advanced economies where industrial concentration has risen. Despite the tremendous promise of artificial intelligence, its productivity dividend has yet to materialize. Without a significant contribution from AI, the burden of aging could be much more severe. Institutional adaptation will matter in other areas as well. While higher worker earnings will help shield public revenues against sharp declines, many industrialized economies already have very high levels of public debt and will need to spend more on pensions and health care as their populations age. This may require new coalitions in which younger generations support spending on public services, including pensions and healthcare, while older generations accept certain pension reforms. Even with these challenges, the available evidence does not support alarmism on either the political or the economic front. The broader social effects could be more complex and bear watching. A shrinking population may change the way people think about the future. In a society with fewer and fewer children, people may become increasingly indifferent to future challenges. That indifference could pose social risks we cannot yet foresee. Strong, flexible institutions may help societies, especially in democracies, articulate and act on new aspirations for the future. Future generations may be smaller, but we still owe it to them to do our best to leave them a healthy, peaceful planet. The choices that will shape that inheritance are already before us. The work must start today. TIME Ideas hosts the world's leading voices, providing commentary on events in news, society, and culture. We welcome outside contributions. Opinions expressed do not necessarily reflect the views of TIME editors.

How to Keep an Open Mind
2026-11-07

How to Keep an Open Mind

TIME | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings No one particularly likes to have their views challenged—to be told they don’t see things clearly or have the full picture. And when someone, like a friend, neighbor, co-worker, journalist, or podcast host disagrees with our way of thinking, it’s natural to get a bit defensive. It’s easy to think we are right, and someone else’s perceptions and ways of being are wrong. But the tighter we clench our jaws and fists, the more we restrict our ability to connect and expand both our views and our connections. Open-mindedness is related in part to intellectual humility, recognizing one’s own imperfections as a knower, and appreciating others’ thinking strengths. Indeed, those who recognize that their beliefs or opinions may be fallible or incomplete are often more willing to learn about others’ views. Research shows that taking in new information helps us widen our knowledge base, increases creativity and psychological well-being, enhances professional development, facilitates empathy, improves relationships, and widens social networks. When we only stay within our comfortable social media algorithms and tight in-person circles, we can get stuck in echo chambers, reinforcing what we already think we know. While this can provide identity and a sense of belonging, it can limit our exposure to new experiences. In this way, it is in our best interest to be willing to listen to different points of view, to fully hear someone else’s perspective before flinging verdicts on their intellect or integrity or viewing them as uninformed and biased. Even reading this suggestion to consider being more open-minded may increase your anxiety and create tension in your body. But I’m not trying to change your mind per se; you just need to be open to expanding it. In an ideal world, we want to aim to increase what psychologists call our “latitude of acceptance,” meaning a willingness to non-defensively consider ideas that others hold. Doing so does not guarantee we are going to change our minds, but it does show that we are ready to listen and consider others’ perspectives. If we simply hear what other people have to say but have no intention of expanding our hearts and minds, then we remain entrenched and defended. That’s not openness. A willingness to consider others’ perspectives can be difficult in general, and particularly hard when the subject at hand is something that is near and dear to our hearts, such as religion and politics. If we are willing and motivated to practice being more open-minded, and if we want to set ourselves up for success, it might be easier to pick topics where we feel less defensive: Are dogs or cats better companions? Is it more enjoyable to watch a newly released film at home or in the cinema? Are you for or against pineapple on pizza? These topics may sound silly, but they invite dialogue and healthy conversation and disagreement. It also helps if we assume that people with alternative views from our own have good intentions. When we vilify people who think differently than we do, we are less able to be tolerant and open to understanding. If there is one thing that I stress to my patients above all others, it’s the ability to loosen our grip in thinking we know everything and have all the answers. We’re human, so it’s not possible or even realistic to expect that every belief we hold is accurate. We may not have all the facts or understand all the circumstances. I encourage the people I work with to put on their detective caps and determine if what they are thinking is true. This includes their unhelpful thinking about themselves, other people, and the world. It also includes how they interpret situations. I try to model open-mindedness, gathering data, looking at perspectives from different angles, and weighing the information fairly. I asked Damion Grasso, a professor of psychiatry at the University of Connecticut, how he practices being open-minded in both his professional and personal life. He told me that in working with trauma-exposed youth and families, “Open-mindedness is essential. It helps me set aside assumptions, stay aware of my own biases, and create a therapeutic space where young people and caregivers feel heard, respected, and actively involved in the process of change.” Grasso told me that in parenting, open-mindedness is also helpful. “Each of my four children has their own story, needs, values, and aspirations,” he said. “My job is to listen more than I speak and remember that I’m still learning too. I don’t have all the answers, and accepting that makes me both a better psychologist and father.” I agree. Receptivity to alternative viewpoints is a wonderful attribute to practice and model for our children. So how do we strengthen our ability to be open minded? A comprehensive review of interventions to increase open-mindedness revealed several effective approaches. One can be thought of as awareness training: teaching people about cognitive biases, for example confirmation bias, which is the inclination for people to trust too much in their preferred assumptions and to take in new information that fits their existing beliefs. Another is perspective-taking interventions where one tries to imagine another person’s experiences and why they might think in a certain way. This can be enhanced by viewing a picture, video, or recording of the other person or using virtual reality technology to powerfully see their perspective. These strategies seem to work particularly well when we meet another person and share our perspectives on their thinking. Another powerful tool is perspective-getting, which involves non-confrontationally asking another to share their outlooks. People can also learn to develop skills to navigate difficult conversations, such as engaging face-to-face rather than side by side, asking open-ended, curious questions, and using receptive language, such as expressing uncertainty, acknowledging mistakes, and finding points of agreement. Of course, these interventions don’t always work and can backfire, making some even more entrenched in their beliefs. And while it is in our best interest to consider others’ beliefs, this is not always the case. Some views may go deeply against our core values, and that’s okay if we choose not to bend on those. But we can still benefit from being gracious and respectful toward others. Imagine our workplaces, home life, and neighborhoods engaged in being more curious, reflective, and respectful. With an open-minded perspective, we could learn and accomplish many things together. TIME Ideas hosts the world's leading voices, providing commentary on events in news, society, and culture. We welcome outside contributions. Opinions expressed do not necessarily reflect the views of TIME editors.

How Europe's Far-Right Leaders Are Backing Away From Trump After Midterm Losses
2026-11-06

How Europe's Far-Right Leaders Are Backing Away From Trump After Midterm Losses

TIME | 2026-11-06

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Alice Weidel, co-leader of the far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) party, speaks ahead of a parliamentary group meeting in the Bundestag in Berlin on Oct. 6, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Europe’s far-right leaders have shown signs of distancing themselves from President Donald Trump after his Republican Party suffered significant blows in the midterm elections, losing control of both the House and Senate. The shift, although slight, is notable considering how Europe’s right-leaning figures have previously celebrated sharing ideological ties with Trump. When the now-embattled President returned to the White House in January 2025, Italian Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni and Santiago Abascal, the leader of Spain's far-right Vox party, were among the European lawmakers invited to his Inauguration Day. But the tide may now be turning. While left-leaning European leaders have celebrated the Democrats’ gains in the U.S. midterms, some far-right figures in Europe appear keen to succinctly separate themselves from Trump and his policies. Trump ran in 2024 on a campaign that promised to avoid dragging the U.S. into foreign wars, instead focusing on home prosperity. His war with Iran, which is now in its ninth month, has gone against that campaign pledge—a point Alice Weidel, co-leader of the far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) party, brought up in her assessment of the GOP’s midterm losses. "Trump had promised his voters he would not pursue an interventionist foreign policy," Weidel told Germany’s T-online outlet on Wednesday. "This broken campaign promise has now come back to haunt the Republicans." While Weidel acknowledged that the political party in control of the White House typically loses seats in Congress during the midterms, she said that the scale of the Republican defeat “is remarkable” and can be explained primarily by the “devastating consequences of the Iran war." The commentary marked a shift in tone for a party that has often welcomed the support of the Trump Administration, both overtly and covertly. Weidel met with Vice President J.D. Vance in February 2025, just days before the German election. During that trip, Vance spoke at the Munich Security Conference and took time out of his speech to criticize what is known as the “firewall” approach in Germany—the agreement among other political parties that they will not work with the AfD. In another show of support, billionaire Elon Musk, while heading the Trump Administration’s now-defunct Department of Government Efficiency (DOGE), made a virtual appearance at an AfD campaign event. After the AfD won a state election in September, Trump, who has long accused European countries of holding weak immigration policies, remarked: “The populist party in Germany just had a really big night. They finally got tired of the absolutely horrible immigration rules and regulations, which have hurt Germany.” Further efforts to create distance from the MAGA brand came from Jordan Bardella, president of France’s far-right National Rally. When asked about the Republicans’ midterm losses by a French radio show, Bardella said the “American intervention in Iran" had carried “very heavy consequences” for world economies. “What is certain is that Donald Trump's second term differs quite significantly, in its philosophy and foreign policy, from his first, during which his political ambition was to withdraw the United States from major global conflicts,” he said. Georgios Samaras, assistant professor of public policy at King’s College London, tells TIME that the scale of the Republican defeat sent “shock waves across the continent.” “After two years of catastrophic economic management, [Trump’s] international standing is gone. It's practically like the far-right [is] losing their global leader,” he says, adding that Trump’s loss is likely to be seen as “a gift to all liberals.” In Spain, Abascal, who will take part in a snap election at the end of November, also backed away from any midterms discussion. “I'm not in Michigan to analyze an election, I'm in Ciudad Real. And considering the November 29th elections, I couldn't care less about what happens in other countries,” he told reporters. His positioning stood in stark contrast to the U.S. alliance he nurtured last year, when Trump during the 2025 Conservative Political Action Conference praised Abascal for doing a “great job” in Spain. In 2022, Trump made a virtual appearance at one of Abascal's rallies in Madrid. When approached about how Europe's far-right leaders have reacted to the GOP's midterm losses, White House deputy press secretary Anna Kelly told TIME: “President Trump has effectively restored American strength on the global stage and strengthened relationships with world leaders.” Aurélien Mondon, a professor of politics at the University of Bath who specializes in reactionary politics, points to how far-right parties in Europe have benefited from “transnational cooperation” with the MAGA movement. That cooperation has spanned years, ranging from Trump ally Steve Bannon’s efforts to bolster right-wing parties in Europe to the Trump Administration’s antagonistic posture toward Europe’s mainstream political parties and leaders. Trump’s influence has also extended to political campaigns, with his ally in Britain, Reform UK leader Nigel Farage, staging MAGA-style rallies during last year’s local elections in England. “These kinds of transnational networks have served [the far-right] well in many ways, and it allowed them to mainstream their politics,” says Mondon, who warns that such alliances can also be limited and opportunistic. “At the end of the day, these parties are about the nation, and so they can very easily detach themselves from the failures of their counterparts elsewhere.” The growing distance between Trump and some of his far-right European allies is not entirely new. Mujtaba Rahman, managing director for Europe at Eurasia Group, a political-risk consultancy, says that “far-right leaders across the European continent have been distancing themselves from MAGA for quite some time.” “It's been clear to different persuasions of the far-right in the E.U. that association with the Trump brand, the MAGA brand, is an electoral liability,” he says. “That happened in the aftermath of Trump's Greenland pronouncements, and the aftermath of the war in Iran, and the impact that's having on cost-of-living issues, which is a key political issue.” In 2024, Bardella said his “heart leans toward Trump,” but he has since criticized some of the President’s policies. In January, when Trump ramped up his threats to annex Greenland, Bardella urged the European Union to unite against what he referred to as “a direct challenge to the sovereignty of a European country.” AfD leadership also distanced itself from the annexation rhetoric. Meloni said Trump’s threats to impose tariffs on European allies over Greenland would be a “mistake.” Beyond differences over foreign policy, Meloni, once dubbed Europe’s “Trump Whisperer,” also clashed with the President in June after he said she had “begged” for a photo with him at the G7 Summit—a claim Meloni said was “completely fabricated.” The Iran war, in particular, has deepened tensions between the Trump Administration and some of Europe’s far-right leaders, as the conflict has hit European markets with soaring energy costs, adding to cost-of-living pressures across the continent. Matteo Salvini, Italy’s deputy Prime Minister and leader of the far-right League party, said he hoped the Republicans’ midterm losses would be interpreted as a halt “to interventionism around the world.” He added: “The American result was influenced above all by the wars, international tensions and the increase in the cost of living, ​the consequences of which fall directly on families.” Rupert Lowe, leader of the far-right Restore Britain party, similarly argued that Trump’s war with Iran led to the Republicans’ election defeats, referring to the conflict as a “catastrophic” mistake. “What has this war achieved? Absolutely nothing of worth,” he said, without directly naming Trump in his reflections. While these tensions had been building for months, Rahman argues that the midterm results have further “tarnished the MAGA brand.” “I think [Europe’s far-right] were hoping the Trump Administration would legitimize their own political identity. It would produce a tailwind that would help them, in the context of elections next year,” he says, pointing to France’s upcoming presidential election in 2027. Trump hailed far-right figure Marine Le Pen as a “conservative voice” amid the protests in France last month. Le Pen was convicted for misusing E.U. funds last year. An appeals court upheld the conviction in July, but shortened her sentence, clearing the path for her to run in the April 2027 presidential election. She is currently leading in polls ahead of the high-stakes race. When asked if he plans to endorse Le Pen, Trump told reporters in the Oval Office: "Well, I don't know her, I haven't met her. But I thought she was badly treated. It would look like a weaponization to me.” The Trump Administration has previously engaged in some of Europe’s pivotal elections. In Hungary, Viktor Orbán, who had received a strong endorsement from Trump, was joined on the campaign trail by Vice President J.D. Vance. The display of U.S. strength did little to sway the tide, as Orbán was defeated by Péter Magyar in April, bringing an end to his consequential 16-year rule—and marking the loss of a key European ally for Trump. But will the U.S. presence be so sought after in Europe moving forward? “I think the conclusion, given the chaos, the turbulence, and incompetence of the Administration, is actually that MAGA and Trump are a liability, and if there is any association, it is better to be covert, not overt,” argues Rahman. Meanwhile, Mondon says that while outward cooperation between the Trump Administration and far-right parties in Europe may diminish, it is unlikely to disappear entirely. "I don't think they will radically distance themselves from Trump or anything like this,” he says. “But I think they will cool down the kind of links between the U.S. and their own parties and countries, at least [for] the moment.”

All at Once, Ted Cruz Is the Last Statewide Republican Lawmaker Standing in Texas
2026-11-06

All at Once, Ted Cruz Is the Last Statewide Republican Lawmaker Standing in Texas

TIME | 2026-11-06

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In the wake of a blue wave that has washed Republicans around the U.S. out of office, Sen. Ted Cruz will soon be the only remaining member of the GOP to hold a statewide-elected political seat in the recently deep-red state of Texas. “2026 was not simply a blue wave, it was a blue tsunami,” the three-term Senator said on his podcast following Election Day, when a series of stunning Democratic victories shifted control of both chambers of Congress, as well as Texas’ executive branch and state House, out of Republicans’ hands. “Texas is blue. That is a hard sentence to utter.” Cruz was not up for reelection this midterm season, but every Republican who was on the ballot in Texas’ statewide races this year has been defeated. Incumbent John Cornyn, Texas’ other Republican Senator, lost in his party’s primary to Attorney General Ken Paxton, who went on to lose in the general election to James Talarico. Talarico is the first Democrat the state has elected to the Senate since 1988. Democrat Gina Hinojosa shattered another decades-long streak of Republican victories by beating out incumbent Greg Abbott in Texas’ gubernatorial race, becoming the state’s first-ever Latina governor and the first Democrat elected to the post since the 1990s. The party’s wins in Texas cut beyond those top positions, as well; Democrats on Tuesday claimed multiple seats in the state’s delegation in the U.S. House, as well as its lieutenant governor, attorney general, comptroller, land commissioner, and agricultural commissioner offices; four state Supreme Court seats; a seat on the state’s railroad commission; and control of the lower house of the Texas legislature. Read More: What Just Happened in Texas Changes Everything “Nowhere are the consequences of this election greater than Texas. In the Lone Star State, we lost every single statewide election,” Cruz said on his show. He noted that he had reached out to Talarico to congratulate him on his win and to tell him that he looks forward to working together. He vowed, however, to “defend Texas” if what he referred to as the “newly elected radicals” attempt to raise taxes or relax border policy, among other issues. Read More: Talarico Wins Texas Senate Race, First Democrat to Win Statewide in More than 30 Years Cruz said that Republicans did not do enough in their campaigns to address Americans’ concerns over the cost of living stemming from the war in Iran. “Tariffs have made everything too expensive, and the Iran war has dragged on too long,” he said. “Understandably, people were pissed off, and elected Republicans didn't do enough to address their very real pain.” Cruz, who campaigned heavily for Republicans leading up to the midterms, first won his Senate seat in 2012, when he beat out Democrat Paul Sadler by 16 percentage points. He was re-elected by a far slimmer margin in 2018, however, defeating Democratic Rep. Beto O’Rourke by only 2.5 points. That year, Trump’s first midterms as president, Republicans gained two Senate seats to expand their majority, but lost the lower chamber, in which Democrats gained 41 seats. In his most recent Senate battle in 2024, a presidential election year that saw Trump win Texas by nearly 14 points, Cruz safely defeated Democrat Colin Allred by an 8.5 point margin. Despite the sweeping defeats his party suffered in the state, Cruz remained optimistic that Texas would return to its prior ruby-red status. “Texas will be red again,” he said. “It is dark right now, but it is always darkest before the dawn. And morning is coming. That is my fervent prayer. And that is my commitment to you.”

Why The Democrats' Next Climate Agenda Will Look Different
