www.theregister.com - Articles
PyTorch 2.15 tackles the 'one bad GPU' problem
2026-11-06

PyTorch 2.15 tackles the 'one bad GPU' problem

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-11-06

Visit Original Website
Technical debt shoveled aside to make large-scale AI training more resilient ML admins will be happy to learn that one bad GPU may no longer mean starting an entire training job from scratch. The latest 2.15 release of PyTorch includes a rewritten backend for Nvidia's Collective Communications Library (NCCL), designed to improve fault diagnosis and recovery when GPUs work together. This latest feature shows how its creators continue to advance the software from being a tool for researchers to being a cornerstone in production AI work. The interface to the networking library in PyTorch needed a rewrite – it was drowning in technical debt, said PyTorch core developer Tristan Rice, a Meta software engineer specializing in distributed computing, during a live YouTube livestream on Thursday. NCCL is pronounced "Nickel" and stands for the Nvidia Collective Communications Library. It provides PyTorch with a set of inter-GPU communication primitives, allowing GPUs to communicate within a single-node or across multi-node clusters, which is vital for running inference on large neural networks and for other tasks in building and maintaining LLMs. The previous PyTorch backend for NCCL, called ProcessGroupNCCL, could spawn dozens of communicators per process group, leading to intermittent deadlocks and unpredictable performance. The new version of the NCCL backend comes with a strict rule: One process group gets only one communicator during initialization. By default, this rule eliminates crosstalk between multiple communicators and all the complexities that entails. If a program requires parallelism, it will have to dispatch more process groups. The updated library also better handles stragglers by allowing for easier reconfiguration. If a GPU crashes or runs slowly, a third-party orchestrator can exclude the laggard and even assign a replacement worker. A “flight recorder” has also been added to give admins a better idea of what went wrong. Previously, if a cluster of GPUs stalled—perhaps from a timeout or deadlock—the user would just get a generic message. The new flight recorder captures all operations in a series of snapshots that can be inspected after a failure occurs. Rice noted the cleaned-up NCCL backend, also called ProcessGroupNCCL, will give developers a blank slate, making it easier to add new features. The legacy ProcessGroupNCCL is part of the 2.15 package as a rollback, but if the new NCCL backend works as expected, the older version will be removed in the 2.16 release, planned for early next year. Why, you may ask, does a machine learning framework need a specialized communications library? Like NumPy, PyTorch supports numerical computing, but it also provides tools for building and training neural networks on CPUs and GPUs. In these environments, workers must constantly exchange data such as tensors, gradients, activations, and weights. The standard TCP-based networking stack can introduce bottlenecks in large GPU clusters, so NCCL uses optimized communication paths to move data between GPUs while reducing CPU overhead. Nvidia initially released NCCL at SC15 as a general-purpose accelerator, and it was adopted by the Facebook folks when they first built PyTorch in 2016. Their goal was to build a Python-based version of the Lua-based Torch ML library. “Every major ML lab (OpenAI, DeepMind, Meta, Nvidia) uses PyTorch. It powers everything from diffusion models and LLMs to recommender systems and robotics. This widespread adoption isn’t accidental,” noted Will Stewart, co-founder of AI software provider Northflank, in a blog post. Other new features of PyTorch 2.15 include a new Python transport API for asynchronous one-way data movement – ideal for training jobs where weights are synchronized between trainers and generators – as well as parallel processing improvements for Apple gear and for supporting Nvidia’s next-generation Blackwell GPUs. Overall, the update consisted of 3,117 commits from 583 contributors. ® The case for managed inference, or automatically routing queries to the right model for the right job Team given a day's notice faced distressed callers, safeguarding decisions during project that stretched from 2 weeks to 11 PARTNER CONTENT: 300+ AI applications delivering surges in core metrics and annual cost reduction of over CNY100m Bad drivers behind the wheel or underneath the operating system – neither is ideal If you wouldn't put it on GitHub, don't give models access to it Technical debt shoveled aside to make large-scale AI training more resilient Agent orchestration startup gets its exit Firms still want kit on-premises, but spike in revenue is as much about increased costs as higher sales Customers sweating assets amid tight budgets, as Microsoft execs get creative with sales pitches But first it needs planning permission, government approval and, rather importantly, a customer PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' The Verge
The UV-tracking Gem made me rethink how sunlight affects my health, not just my appearance
2026-11-07

The UV-tracking Gem made me rethink how sunlight affects my health, not just my appearance

The Verge | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
What started as a test to understand how sun exposure was aging my skin took a turn that I wasn’t expecting. What started as a test to understand how sun exposure was aging my skin took a turn that I wasn’t expecting. With temperatures rising to the 90s in Los Angeles, avoiding the heat wasn’t exactly an option while I was at a relative’s outdoor party in August. So I sat beneath a patio umbrella with a powerful fan nearby, figuring I was protected from the sun and heat. Half an hour later, I felt exhausted, dizzy, nauseous, and generally unwell, symptoms consistent with heat exhaustion. Around the same time, I received a notification from an app used by the $249 ($149 at the time of publication) UV-tracking necklace I’d been wearing, called Gem, from a company called The90. It warned that my UV exposure had climbed considerably. It turns out UV radiation wasn’t repelled by this patio umbrella, and it’s hard to ever be fully protected because radiation can be reflected or scattered by objects in the environment. While UV exposure and overheating are different things, I’d mistakenly assumed the shade and the fan would adequately protect me from both. This led to a discovery: One of my medications increases my sensitivity to heat, which helped to explain similar episodes I’d experienced over the years. The Gem didn’t diagnose anything, but it prompted me to investigate something I might otherwise have dismissed. Since then, I’ve been more mindful about limiting time outdoors during hours of peak heat and UV, staying hydrated, and paying closer attention to how I’m feeling outside. I’ll admit my initial interest in tracking UV was largely vanity-driven. As much as I try to challenge harmful beauty standards, I’m hardly immune to them. Having started going gray in my 20s, I’m keen on slowing down other visible signs of aging where I reasonably can. But between being Indian (I rarely get sunburned thanks in part to my skin’s higher melanin content, not that it eliminates the risk of sun damage or skin cancer) and living within walking distance of the beach in perpetually sunny LA, I hadn’t given much thought to how much UV exposure I was actually getting. The Gem has helped me better understand it by providing a range of estimates, some more useful than others. The included UV-detecting sensor measures UVA and UVB radiation separately, rather than simply displaying my area’s UV forecast. UVB is primarily responsible for sunburn and helps your skin produce vitamin D, while UVA contributes to premature aging, even when you aren’t visibly burning. Thankfully, getting these metrics doesn’t come at the cost of wearing a bulky device. The circular pendant comes with a cute, thin necklace I’d happily wear even if it didn’t track anything. Its titanium, gold- or silver-colored chain pairs well with a variety of outfits. Battery life lasted almost exactly a week (the advertised lifespan), and the Gem recharges using an included charging dock. The90 plans to release a hat clip and bag tag early next year, which seem ideal for those who prefer not to wear it around their neck. The mobile app is central to the experience. It has easy-to-read graphs and jargon-light explanations. I enjoyed using it. While not required, you can create a skin profile so that it can estimate a range of metrics based on the sensor’s UVA and UVB readings. The profile is based upon factors like skin type, sensitivities, clothing, and sunscreen. That means its estimates aren’t based on UV exposure alone, though they’re still predictions rather than direct measurements of how your body is responding to sunlight. There’s a lot here, including your estimated vitamin D production, potential circadian light benefits, estimated nitric oxide-related benefits, cumulative UVA exposure, and recent accumulated UVA exposure. Alongside cumulative UVA exposure and weekly trends, its Skin Aging Zone assesses potential aging-related risk and offers personalized skincare advice. It also provides a personalized sunburn countdown, sunscreen reminders, and a daily Sun Score. The Sun Score is a rating that incorporates various metrics to balance sunlight’s potential benefits against the risks of overexposure. The company encourages users to aim for a daily Sun Score of 90, though more sun isn’t necessarily better. Too little exposure means missing potential benefits, while excessive UV can damage your skin. I appreciated the context, but the individual estimated measurements proved more revealing than the Sun Score. The UV readings in the app responded quickly as I moved between sunlight, shade, and indoors, even registering changes near windows. I couldn’t independently verify the sensor’s accuracy, but the readings behaved as expected as I shifted between environments. However, there were times when I noticed the Gem sometimes failed to register that I had been outside. Eventually, I discovered the app needs to remain running in the background (at least on iPhone; I haven’t tested on Android), as measurements aren’t stored on the sensor, and fully closing the app interrupts tracking. My experience at the outdoor party challenged my assumptions about how much protection I was getting from the shade. Just as surprising to me was learning how little UVB I accumulated during a typical week, despite living somewhere so sunny. I fell short of the app’s estimated sunlight-derived vitamin D target almost daily, prompting me to research how melanin, while offering some natural UV protection, impacts vitamin D production from sunlight. Until then, I’d mostly thought about sun exposure in terms of protecting my skin from damage. Seeing how little UVB I was accumulating made me consider what I might be missing out on, too. The vitamin D countdown wasn’t as helpful as I had hoped. During one test, its estimate of how much additional sunlight I needed dropped from several hours to under an hour, then climbed past six hours just minutes later. The fluctuations appeared related to changing UVB intensity, though I couldn’t verify that. I noticed similar fluctuations in the app’s estimated time to sunburn. With both countdowns changing so dramatically, I stopped paying much attention to the exact times and focused instead on the broader picture: whether it felt like I was getting too much or too little sun. That was perhaps the biggest surprise of testing the Gem. After my experience using it at the outdoor party in August, I’d expected it just would encourage me to spend less time in the sun each day. In reality, my exposure often falls at one of two extremes: either too little to meet the app’s estimated targets or enough to raise concerns about overexposure, even when I thought I was protected by the shade. I now try to be more mindful about my UV exposure, even when I’m not using the Gem. I try to limit time spent outdoors during peak UV hours, and I try to get some sunlight on days spent mostly indoors. I credit the Gem with helping me better understand how my time outside could affect my well-being in ways I’d never considered, though the usefulness of your findings may vary. At $249 outside of a deal, it’s expensive, though it’s nice that it doesn’t have a subscription. I personally can’t justify the price given how much time I spend indoors, but I see the appeal for people who spend considerable time outside, or those who want to be more mindful of sun protection. For the price, I wish the app’s guidance was clearer at times, and I wish it could track UV without the app being open in the background, but the Gem ultimately got me thinking less about how my body looks and more about how I’m treating it. Discogs’ Kevin Lewandowski can’t live without his AeroPress

Discogs’ Kevin Lewandowski can’t live without his AeroPress

The Verge | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
The founder of Discogs talks coffee, Rez, and (of course) records. The founder of Discogs talks coffee, Rez, and (of course) records. Twenty-five years ago, Kevin Lewandowski founded Discogs, arguably the destination online for obsessive vinyl collectors and music nerds of all stripes. It is a marketplace for buying and selling records, obviously, but it’s also become so much more. It’s a shockingly comprehensive database that covers not just albums and singles, but all the various versions and pressings. It’s a really fun rabbit hole to disappear down when you’re trying to figure out what obscure pressing of Ministry’s The Mind is a Terrible Thing to Taste or Psalm 69 you had in high school, because you definitely blasted the cover of “Lay Lady Lay” all the time and 100 percent never owned Filth Pig. In the years since it debuted, Discogs has grown to be much more than just a site where audiophiles go to trade rare first pressings of Beach Boys records. There are forums, groups, user-created lists, and more. It’s part eBay, part Reddit, and IMDB — just specifically for music. With more and more people turning their back on the algorithm and rediscovering physical media, now seemed like a perfect time to check in with Lewandowski. My AeroPress. I use it multiple times a day. In fact, I just made a coffee with it [sips coffee]. How many tabs do you have open right now? Probably around 30 on my work computer. My personal computer is worse — probably 40 or 50. Which social media platform do you use the most? I look at X and Instagram occasionally, but I don’t really post. What is your favorite gadget you’ve ever owned? It’s not really a gadget, but I like to tinker. Lately I’ve been playing around with Home Assistant software and built my own setup to control things like the garage door and outdoor lights. So I guess my favorite gadget is one I made myself. What is one thing you wish you had created? Probably a game like Rez, which came out around 2000. It’s a rhythm game where you’re almost controlling the music as you play, about as close to DJing as a video game can get. I’ve actually had an idea for my own version with a similar concept. It’s still just an idea, though. I’ll either clear everything off my desk or change locations completely. Go to a coffee shop or sit outside. Sometimes I just need a different environment. What’s the last piece of physical media you bought? Records. I was at Mint Gallery in Portland last week and picked up four. I still really like going into a record store and finding something to bring home. And I also recently picked up three reissues from the Moodsville / Acoustic Sounds series. What would the tagline for your biopic be? I don’t use memes very often, but one I was kind of proud of was putting a Discogs screen into a North Korean war room photo. NYT Cooking’s AI assistant saved my weeknight chicken, but almost ruined my cake
2026-11-07

NYT Cooking’s AI assistant saved my weeknight chicken, but almost ruined my cake

The Verge | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
I tested the new AI-powered tool on ingredient swaps, recipe scaling, and a missing Bundt pan. Around 7PM each weeknight, I find myself standing in front of the fridge, trying to decide on something for dinner that won’t make my husband complain, “Chicken again?” Yes, I should meal plan more, but I don’t, so this is where I’m at. A nightly battle in which the chicken usually wins. Recently, my best weapon in this fight has been AI. Between Alexa Plus in the Echo smart display on my kitchen counter, the Samsung Food app on my fridge, and even an AI-powered cooking robot, AI has become my go-to sous chef — albeit one I need to keep a close eye on. It’s helped me swap ingredients, generate ideas, fix things when I mess up, and yes, even cook better. So I was intrigued when my go-to recipe app, NYT Cooking, introduced an AI assistant. Many, including some NYT Cooking users and recipe purists, are skeptical of this new tool (available to subscribers on iOS only at launch), and I get why. One of the things I like most about NYT Cooking is that it features thousands of recipes from dozens of reputable sources — such as the New York Times archives, restaurants, cookbooks, and food columnists — and real people have tested them. (I’ve tried some purely AI-generated recipes and they were not good). Another great feature of the service, which I’ve been paying for for several years, is the reader comments. Here, I’ve often found solutions to cooking dilemmas, like what to substitute for makrut lime leaves — there’s always one slightly bougie ingredient in an NYT Cooking recipe that I don’t have on hand. However, reading through hundreds of comments to find answers at 7PM on a weeknight isn’t always practical. And this is where I’ve found the NYT Cooking assistant helpful. Among other things, it was trained on user comments, and in my few days testing it, it provided practical substitutions and solid cooking guidance for the new ingredients. It also won’t unquestionably agree to crazy requests (I tried!). As with all AI tools, it’s not perfect — it almost made me put half the required butter in my cake — but as a tool, it is useful and feels like a solid application of AI. Importantly, it doesn’t change the existing recipe; it leaves it as is and creates a new one for you. It doesn’t change the existing recipe; it leaves it as is and creates a new one for you The assistant appears in the app in a small text box on the recipe page with a prompt: “Customize this recipe…” From here, you type in a question to adapt it to your needs. This can be as simple as “What can I substitute for this ingredient?” to “Scale this recipe to feed seven people, and make it vegan and gluten-free.” The New York Times has also said the feature can suggest substitutions for cooking equipment and convert measurements; the latter option has been in the app for a couple of years. Last Friday night, instead of staring aimlessly into the fridge, I turned to the Chicken folder in my NYT Cooking app Recipe Box and found a One-Pot Chicken and Rice With Ginger I’d been wanting to try. Only I didn’t have chicken thighs or half the ingredients. I could have figured these steps myself, but it was nice to have validation that my modifications wouldn’t make the dish taste terrible. I typed my dilemma into the assistant box on the recipe page: “I have chicken tenders, fresh ginger, garlic, rice, broth, limes, but no chard or cilantro or scallions. I do have green beans and red onions.” Within seconds, it created a new version of the recipe, with a new title: One-Pot Chicken and Rice With Ginger and Green Beans. It suggested a shorter browning time for the tenders, cooking the red onion alongside the chicken, and steaming the green beans on top of the rice for the last eight minutes instead of the chard. Every change was highlighted in the new recipe under a Your Version tab, and I could flip back to the original at any point. I noticed it hadn’t adjusted for the lack of cilantro, saying it was optional. So I asked if I could use Thai basil, which I happen to have, and it agreed, highlighting how it would change the flavor. While I could have figured out a lot of these steps myself, maybe with the help of Google, it was nice to have validation that my modifications wouldn’t make the dish taste terrible and nice not to have to swipe out of the app. The NYT Cooking editors say it’s been trained not to comply with requests that will taste bad. The resulting dish — which took the same amount of time to cook as the original — was delicious and got my husband’s seal of approval (he just had the leftovers for lunch). It was very different from the recipe Yasmin Fahr wrote, and I can see the purists’ point. But the rewritten recipe was much easier to use and produced a better dish than winging it with my own subs gleaned from the comments section, which I usually do with varying degrees of success. I cook regularly, so I had a sense of what ingredients I had on hand that might work for the dish I wanted to make. But the assistant can also help if you have no idea what to use as a substitute. For example, I was cooking a Thai red curry and asked what I could use instead of makrut lime leaf. It replied, saying that grated lime zest would work, but “it misses the deeper floral, herbal aroma” of fresh limes. (See? Bougie.) Over the weekend, I tried several more swaps, asking whether half-and-half would work instead of cream in a caramel apple Dutch baby, or if Castelvetrano olives and smoked paprika could stand in for kalamata and chile pepper in a roasted halibut dish. Swapping spices is always a bold move in any dish, as it can really ruin the flavor. I also had the app convert the Dutch baby recipe’s metric weights to US measurements, which it did seamlessly. Then I threw in some curveballs. How about using fresh cayenne peppers with the halibut? It warned me that substituting cayenne for chile “brings significant, sharp heat.” Could I swap ladyfingers for minced beef in this trifle (à la Rachel Green in Friends)? “Minced beef cannot be used in place of the ladyfingers or anywhere in this sweet dessert trifle.” Good call. While I was cooking, I compared the NYT Cooking assistant with Alexa Plus, Amazon’s generative-AI voice assistant I regularly use to help me cook via the Echo Show 11 smart display in my kitchen. Interestingly, it offered the same guidance as the Cooking assistant on substitutions for the chicken and rice dish, but with slightly different cooking times. Alexa pulls some recipes from the NYT app through a partnership between the companies. However, Alexa couldn’t show the modified recipe on its screen; it could only tell me the changes while showing the original. When I asked it to walk me through them, it got confused and improvised, telling me to cut the chicken into bite-sized pieces — which wasn’t in the modified recipe. Overall, I found using the Cooking app more reliable, although messier. It would be great to be able to use voice control to navigate the app, as I can with Alexa on my smart display, rather than using my garlicky fingers. I am typically not a baker. My cakes come out flat, and my cookies don’t crumble. Even the smart mixer I tested a few years ago couldn’t save me. I also rarely have the right equipment. So I took Cinnamon Roll Bundt Cake, a recipe by NYT Cooking editor Genevieve Ko (who announced the AI feature), and asked it to turn it into muffins. (Who has a Bundt cake pan?!) It modified the recipe’s cooking time from three hours to 50 minutes and split the batter across two 12-muffin tins. But that was going to make 24 muffins, way too many for me. So I asked it to halve the recipe to get 12 muffins. This led to some smart choices, like halving five eggs to three instead of an unhelpful two and a half. For science, I bought a Bundt pan to make the original version too. Both turned out great, but things got a bit sticky when I swapped between the original recipe and the AI version in the app. You can’t save your new recipe separately, which caused confusion when I was cooking the original. Instead of scrolling back through the recipe with sticky fingers, I asked the assistant how much butter I needed (using voice typing), and it gave me the modified recipe’s 6 tablespoons rather than the original’s three-quarter cup, without telling me which version it was using. You can delete your version, which I would advise doing to avoid issues like this — 6 tablespoons of butter would have made for a dry cake. If you already read all the comments before starting a New York Times recipe, then you will probably find this tool offers a similar experience in a more streamlined way. For everyday problems — wrong cut of chicken, no cilantro, or no Bundt cake pan — it was genuinely helpful and saved me a trip to the store. It was also smart enough to know when to say no to minced beef in a trifle. As with every AI tool I’ve used in the kitchen, the NYT Cooking AI assistant works well as a sous chef, but it’s not the head chef — I still felt the need to double-check amounts and cooking times and cross-reference them with the original to make sure it hadn’t done something outlandish. If you’re a purist, you can just ignore the assistant. While you can’t turn off the chat box, it’s easy to scroll past. I do think it will make me use the app more often, since I no longer need to worry as much when I don’t have makrut lime leaves on hand or the right cut of meat. For me, standing in front of the fridge with chicken tenders and no plan, it’s been a useful addition to my nightly cooking routine. The US let Nvidia sell chips to an empty office in China

The Verge | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
The government is approving sales of Nvidia’s AI chips to Chinese firms with ties to blacklisted companies. The government is approving sales of Nvidia’s AI chips to Chinese firms with ties to blacklisted companies. Concerns are being raised that neither the Commerce Department nor Nvidia are doing their due diligence when it comes to vetting Chinese firms cleared to handle the company’s H200 AI chips. According to The New York Times, New Matrix is on the list of approved firms, yet its offices are empty, “a hollow gray shell with wires dangling from the ceiling where lights should be.” In fact, the paper could find little evidence that the company exists in any meaningful way: Only scant information can be found online about New Matrix, which was created 17 months ago. At the building that New Matrix lists as its address, a company directory does not include the firm. At the nearby property management agency for the industrial park, a receptionist and a manager both said they had no record of the company. What little information can be found about New Matrix is concerning. Its owners have ties to a Chinese private equity firm that has been blacklisted by the US government since 2024. And according to the Times, this is not a rare lapse in oversight, but a troubling pattern of companies connected to entities with national-security-related trade restrictions being approved to purchase Nvidia chips. Nvidia had been restricted from selling chips to China during the Biden presidency. But the Trump administration relaxed those limits after Nvidia agreed to give the government a 15-percent cut of all sales. The company’s CEO, Jensen Huang, also launched an intense lobbying campaign and was apparently targeted by China’s Ministry of State Security in a spying plot.
