The Verge
AI broke the cybersecurity world. What’s next?
2026-11-07

AI broke the cybersecurity world. What’s next?

The Verge | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
Going inside the security ‘dark ages’ with Alex Stamos. Today, I’m talking with Alex Stamos, chief security officer at Cognition and longtime cybersecurity expert, about what a mess cybersecurity is right now. This episode is part one of a special series looking at AI in real life. I’ve always enjoyed talking to Alex because he’s so direct, and you’re going to hear him say that the multibillion-dollar cybersecurity industry as we’ve known it is essentially overdue to the unrelenting, AI-boosted waves of hacks, breaches, and attacks — and this is only the beginning of how much AI is changing security. Verge subscribers, don’t forget you get exclusive access to ad-free Decoder wherever you get your podcasts. Head here. Not a subscriber? You can sign up here. Both private actors and also state actors, like Russia or North Korea, are breaking into systems all over thanks to AI helping them both detect and exploit vulnerabilities all at the same time. It’s got a real frontier feel to it, Alex notes, just like the ’90s, when the whole idea of an always-online world was brand-new. That much disruption means a lot of places where bad guys can get in while everyone else is left scrambling. Alex isn’t a doomer in the long run, but he does think we’re in for a “dark age” while we try to figure out how on earth to adapt to the new era. There’s a lot going on here — we even got all the way to how AI-powered misinformation and slop might be managed on our social networks. Before we get going, do me a favor and subscribe to Decoder on YouTube, where we put out new episodes every Monday and Thursday. Okay: Alex Stamos on how AI is upending cybersecurity. Here we go. This interview has been lightly edited for length and clarity. Alex Stamos, you’re the chief security officer at Cognition. You’re also a lecturer at Stanford University Cyber Policy Center. Welcome back to Decoder. I’m excited to talk to you. It’s been a long time since you and I chatted in what you might call the “content moderation era” of internet policy discourse. I feel like you and I talked a lot about how all that was working, about election security, all those sorts of things. We’re in a new era of big problems on the internet, particularly around cyber defense, cybersecurity. You’ve been thinking about this stuff a lot. You referred to the past few weeks as “security awareness month.” Tell me what’s going on. Just give me the lay of land right now. October is traditionally security awareness month. Lots of companies run events and deal both internally and then tell people, “Change your password, use multiple passwords, use a password manager.” What has happened over the last month is we’ve had a couple of these big bugs, PHP, Node and such. What’s been less discussed is a huge number of companies have been breached. You have a series of AI-related companies, the neoclouds that have been breached in succession. You have the Binance hack, which is the largest heist of anything in human history. You have the Cursor incident where one of the things that’s going on here is these hacks are being chained together. From what we know right now, one of the companies that was broken into was Fireworks, which is a prominent provider of AI services to a number of companies. From the Fireworks hack, API keys were stolen that allowed people to break into Cursor. Once they got into Cursor, they then pushed a bad plugin, which went to 5,000 more companies and that caused 5,000 potential breaches. It’s just this kind of permanent continuous breach that is going on throughout the tech industry. A CISO friend of mine said to a bunch of us, “Is anybody else tired?” And everybody said yes, because effectively we have been at DEFCON 2 for all of October and now going into November. I’m going to ask you some really dumb questions here. Who is behind all these attacks? Is it Russia? Is it North Korea? Do we know? It’s a variety of different groups. It is not just because adversaries have decided, but what has happened is, from my perspective, we are at the start of the takeoff of adversary capability enabled by AI, that the open-weight models have now gotten to the point at which you can automate most of the kill chain. This “kill chain” term is something we stole from the military. In the military, they mean to actually kill something. They’re the steps you do to blow something up. In cyber, the kill chain is the things you do to have an effect on a target. So to steal information, to turn something off, to steal money, to steal Bitcoin. Traditionally, you do research upfront, you look at a piece of software, you find a vulnerability, you write exploits, and you do that all way ahead of time. And then you have a human being go and take that stuff you built and deploy it against either one target — because maybe you’re a government and you’re going against Lockheed Martin or you’re going against another government or you’re a ransomware actor — and you take all that work you did and you deploy it against hundreds or thousands of targets really fast. Now with AI, what’s happening is this kill chain has inverted where you can run an AI system that just goes hog wild and it looks at the targets and it finds the bugs in real time and it writes the exploits in real time and it breaks into these companies. One of the things that’s happening is that these systems are going and they’re so good at finding bugs that they are finding the bugs in real time and writing the exploits in real time. The system that everybody’s talking about is not the only one, but the system we’re talking about is a thing called ARTEX, which is a Chinese open-source tool that anybody can go look at. There’s an English translation on GitHub that you can check out. And if you power it on the back end by something like an obliterated GLM-5.3, it will let you break into a shocking number of organizations. This started with three South Korean banks. This week, about 200 more companies have been hit. I’ve been helping a couple of companies. Cognition is not an incident response company, but we do go in and help people fix their bugs afterwards by using our tools. It is shocking the companies that are getting hit by something you could just download. It has been a long time since you’ve been able to go and just download an open-source tool and break into multi-billion dollar companies. It’s like the ‘90s. That’s- That’s what I was thinking about. I think about Windows in the late ‘90s, early 2000s when it was a security disaster. I started saying this actually in the beginning of this year, that it’s an incredibly exciting and terrifying time, because the last time it felt like this was when I was a much younger man and I would go to the very early DEFCONs. DEFCON these days is like 40,000–60,000 people. Back then [in the 1990s] it was a couple hundred people and it was one track and you’d go in and somebody would give a talk and you’d leave the room and you’d think, “Every company in the world is vulnerable to this attack.” The difference was that the entire world didn’t run on the internet. You could go do bad things, but you couldn’t ruin the world and you certainly couldn’t automate it in the way you can now. It’s back to those days where tools you could just get and build yourself could do crazy things. It’s back to the Wild West days. Back in those days, there were a couple hundred people who had those skills and we were all in one room in Las Vegas and most of them were actually good guys. They didn’t want to be criminals. And now you have a much larger ecosystem of people, many of whom live in places where the law enforcement can’t touch and who have a big economic incentive and have access to these tools. The other big thing is they no longer have to use frontier models that have protections, that have logs that are available to law enforcement. My M5 Mac Ultra Studio finally showed up and I’ve been running a quantized obliterated GLM-5.3 and oh my God, what you can do is incredible. You mentioned the economics of frontier models. The economy of cyber is one of those things that has gotten sophisticated over the years. One of the things that our systems got safer is you could pay the number of people at DEF CON a whole bunch of money to protect all the systems and it was economically smart to pay them to be defenders instead of attackers. I’m just rewinding my own brain through this history. There was a long conversation in our industry about security through obscurity by hiding all the bugs and hiding all the code versus open source and being really transparent. There was an etiquette that developed about how to reveal the bugs and when to give people notification. There were transparency reports and bug bounties. You could call it an etiquette, you could call it an economy. We built a culture around cybersecurity and cyber defense and how the attackers and defenders would operate. What you are describing changes it all for a whole number of reasons, but the one that sticks out to me is the economics of it. I can go buy a Mac Studio, I can get an open-weight model and I can just start and that does not place me inside of the culture. There’s not going to be some old man, an Obi-Wan Kenobi saying, “Here’s how you act like a cybersecurity Jedi.” I’m just going to start doing stuff. Tell me how that’s changing right now. Effectively, the last era of security from my perspective started with the Microsoft, the Bill Gates “trustworthy computing” memo in the early 2000s. And that is when we started designing how we do software security and these big companies, Microsoft and Oracle and then later Amazon and Salesforce and these folks, started hiring ex-hackers. I was part of that generation of the ex-hackers who were at DEF CON and Black Hat and got hired by the companies to go break their stuff. We started coming up with responsible disclosure, bug bounties, consulting companies, so the Foundstone and @stake and then Mandiant and CrowdStrike, these companies grow up and we get this huge industry that is now RSA and Black Hat. This multi-billion dollar security industry grew up in the 2000s and 2010s. That era is over, like you said, because it was based upon a couple of assumptions. One was that there was a hierarchy of skillsets of people who could do this stuff. You had the script kiddies who could download tools to do things and then the next level up of the people who could write those tools and that was a more rarefied skill set. There were the vulnerability researchers and then there were the gods, and that god tier was in the hundreds of people and those are the people who could do the really, really good vulnerability research, who could write the really, really good shell code, who could reverse engineer patches from Microsoft or somebody like that. There was also the assumption that there were a limited number of people who could be operational in an attack. If you wanted to build an attack team, then you have a small R&D component and then you have a much larger operational component. If you were a professional attack team at U.S. Cyber Command, at Russia SVR, at Chinese MSS, that you build the structure of your R&D team of elite people and then your less elite operational people, and that was copied then by the Russian ransomware actors. You have to have a conspiracy of people to do this stuff. A lot of this stuff also fell apart with the invention of Bitcoin because one of the assumptions was that you had to have people who could launder money and Bitcoin meant that nerds could launder money. So that started to change the economics of the entire thing. All this stuff is falling apart because of AI, because the skillset issue is that now script kiddies have a bunch of the skillset of the Dave Vitales and the other cyber gods who could do these things, such that with this Mac that’s sitting on my desktop, I can reverse engineer a Microsoft patch and walk through and then write stable exploit code. It’s not quite there yet to be automatic. You still have to have a bunch of skills, but we’re really, really close. What’s really concerning, and what you see with the ARTEX stuff, is you can now kind of let loose on the operational side. We were never secure. It was all a fantasy. These vulnerabilities have always existed. These companies have never been safe. And that is what we’ve seen over the last decade is these mostly Russian ransomware actors nailing the mid-size companies that most people have never heard of that are actually really important, like some midsize industrial company that makes some widget that we all depend upon. I once did work for the largest distributor of medical and dental supplies in the country, and they got really hit badly by a Russian ransomware actor and then dental offices across the country were canceling appointments. Didn’t even make the news, but it was because they couldn’t get gloves, because they couldn’t get supplies. It’s because a company like that’s got one security guy and a 10 person, 12 person IT team and they’re going up against a 20 person Russian ransomware team, they’re toast. But it’s just because there’s only a handful of Russian teams that could do that. Now two or three Russian kids can have a whole bank of Macs or, if they have the money, they can buy Nvidia H-100s and they can run a ton of agents who do this all day and that is what is changing. I’m sorry, you asked who was doing this. For Binance, it’s North Koreans and they have an entire team where all they do is steal Bitcoin. Other people have gotten out of it because it’s really hard to compete against a foreign intelligence agency that recruits their smartest kids and all they do is steal Bitcoin. They’re extremely good at it. And for the Binance hack, we could talk about it. There’s probably some AI component, but mostly that was actually human intelligence and very, very smart social engineering and the North Koreans are... I mean, you have to give it to them. They’re very, very good at that. But for the other stuff, it looks like for the people who are breaking into the AI companies is what’s called the Chinese cottage access industry, which are small Chinese contractors who break into companies and then sell the information to much larger Chinese companies. That diffusion of skill changing the economics of cyber is really fascinating to me. We ran a headline ages ago called “Attack of the Killer Script Kiddies” at the very beginning of all this, and I feel bad because it is much more suited to now than it was to then, because then it was just a lot of automated mischief. Now — Because you wasted the headline. You wish you held it back. We wasted the headline. Every now and again you get a lightning bolt of a headline. You got to get it out there. It’s a great piece of art. We’ll put it in the show notes, but it feels like the diffusion you’re talking about is not that a bunch of teenagers are going to be even more malicious. It’s a bunch of bad actors with real incentives to do harm are now vastly more capable than they were before. And that doesn’t seem like you can re-jigger the economics of that unless you somehow make it really expensive to do the attacks. I think it’s going to be extremely hard to make it expensive to do the attacks. We’re going to have to lower the cost of defense. You’re completely right to look at this economic problem because what’s happening is attacks are becoming agentic. The cost per token for attackers is going towards zero, and the cost of defense for defenders is staying quite high. It is very important for us to find ways for defenders to have cheaper options. I think that is critical. That’s something I am personally extremely focused on. To be frank, very few people are. There are a couple of problems here. One, there’s a ton of focus on the defensive side of AI on finding and fixing bugs. That’s great. It’s important. But software security as a CISO… I work for an AI software company, Cognition. Even as a CISO of a software product company, software security is 25% of my job. I have all kinds of other responsibilities. And software security is the part of my job that has been both the most revolutionized and the most improved by AI. The other 75% has been helped a bit and in some cases made much worse. There’s so much more we need to do than just hill climb on finding and fixing bugs. There’s been so much less hill climbing on all these other things. And the next area needs to be the operational security side. Wait, give me an example of the other things, the 75%. Okay. So the operational security side is watching your logs, saying, “Ah, somebody is running our techs against us. I am now going to trigger an investigation. I am going to look to see what is going on and I’m going to make an intelligent decision of what I am going to do to respond to this without shutting down the entire company.” That is SOC (security operations center) work. Companies that want to survive this era are going to have to replace human beings who do that work with the first tier and then eventually the second tier with AI. And you can do that with current LLMs. The first tier actually has to be not an LLM. It has to be something much dumber because you’re looking at terabytes or petabytes of logs and it is just completely uneconomical to do that with an actual LLM. We have other things — TF-IDFs, MLBERTs, traditional classifiers — that can operate much faster, but then that sets off an alarm and then you send it to a small LLM and then the small LLM calls its bigger brother and that might call an even bigger brother. But for those models, we need to do a lot more work to optimize them and we need to make them cheap. I have a colleague who works at one of the large security platform companies who said that doing an investigation on one of the big frontier model cyber models costs $900 on their product to do an investigation. That is not going to work when the Russian ransomware guys are either using Macs or they’re using throwaway crypto mining rigs and their marginal cost is zero. This is one of the things we’re working on at Cognition: We train our own models, retrain open weights, and we’re trying to drive this cost way down. It’s something we’ve done, but we have a lot more work to do here and a lot of other people have work to do here. This is where, as an industry, we have to focus on the Pareto frontier between cost and efficiency on cyber because it’s not just bug-finding. The other place here is, people talk about Mythos. Mythos is great, Astra Cyber is great. They’re incredible models, but even the people who have access to it, which is a tiny number of companies, they only scan their most important code. And there are hundreds of billions of lines of code out there that are not the Linux kernel, that are the crap C# and J2E code that was written by Infosys 15 years ago. All of that code is not going to go through a frontier cyber model that costs a gazillion dollars. That’s the kind of stuff that we need to work on cost efficiency for. It feels like you’re describing this cliche that I hear all the time: Everything’s going to get so much worse before it gets totally secure forever. And it doesn’t seem like the model you’re describing leads to that result. We’re not going to fix all the bugs forever in some valley of despair and then always have perfect code. At some point, there’s some cost advantage to the attacker, always. You’re running a company, you can’t write defense that has zero marginal costs. Someone has to do it even if the attackers are so infinite that their marginal cost is effectively zero. So are we going to go through a period where everything is so much worse and then it’s perfect forever? Or how does the cost manage itself out, into balance? I am neither the hardest doomer nor the most positive person about this. I feel like we are not going to be doomed. We’re not all going to die or be uploaded to the singularity in the next two years, nor do I think when things get better, they’re going to be perfect. The metaphor I’ve been using is that we are entering a dark age. Things are going to get more bleak and the responsibility of those of us who work in cybersecurity — especially those of us who work in cybersecurity at the AI companies, and we are a lab if a smaller one compared to the big-L Labs — is to help shorten that dark age. The Dark Ages were bad, but we survived them. And then we had the Enlightenment and the Renaissance, but it’s not like the Renaissance was perfect. It wasn’t utopia and everything was perfect. We will still have hacks. All kinds of bad things happen even when you don’t have a security flaw. There are tons of security breaches that do not require a flaw in the software itself. And we still will have flaws in software, even if LLMs are writing all of them in strong languages and it will take a long period of time to get rid of all the flaws. I do think, however, things will start to get better as the median code becomes code that was written by an LLM in a memory-safe and type-safe language. But the problem is we have a couple of decades of stuff that human beings wrote and that wrote in languages that we should have replaced years ago. My feeling is that to shorten this age, we have to do a couple of things. One is we have to stop to get out of this like we’re just going to find bugs and patch them. A bunch of stuff just needs to be refactored. If you’re looking at your code base and you scan it and you’ve got 10,000 bugs, then probably now we are at the point where rewriting it is actually cheaper. That is my observation from looking at these customers of ours: If you can get a working build, then it is actually much easier to write a whole humongous suite of tests and to spec out how this thing works and then to rebuild it within that spec and those tests securely than it is to find all the bugs and fix them. It actually is. I don’t know what is going on through the mind of the Linux kernel developers, but again, in that talk I gave at the beginning of this year, I posited two possible futures and one was that we’re now at superhuman bug finding. Maybe we just have a pool of superhuman bugs and we’re going to drain it. That’s the good future. But the bad future is every time we get a new model, there’s a new superhuman bug pool that’s a little more superhuman. And unfortunately we seem to be closer to that in that the Linux kernel got a bunch of fixes for Mythos and then Mythos Preview and then we had Mythos 5.1 and there was another pool. And then we got Astra Cyber and we just got another 1,000 Linux kernel patches. It’s not slowing down yet. I don’t think we’re just going to scan our way through the C code of the Linux kernel. There are only two companies, honestly, in the world who could probably do this. It’d be Google or Amazon. I wish instead of the Fuchsia project or rebooting the Google book or the Chromebook again, Google would take all of those incredibly brilliant engineers and just tell them, “Take the Linux kernel and rewrite this in Rust.” That would be their gift to the world. And it would be an incredible advertisement for Gemini, for Carbon or whatever would be like, “Get rid of all the drivers that nobody needs. This thing doesn’t have to run on the 46 DX3 or whatever.” I mean, I know it doesn’t, but you know what I’m saying. And then just release it and call it whatever you want, the G Linux kernel, just like they did this with OpenSSL. And I would run that. Cognition would run that on our servers or have Google and Amazon do it together and it would cost them $100 million, but compared to the societal cost that this is costing us every time we have to patch the Linux kernel and you have to reboot every Linux machine on the entire planet, that’s what has to happen. But for whatever reason, the technology industry has not been able to get together and make these hard decisions of these fundamental things that underlie the entire human race, we have to completely rebuild. This is why I started by talking about how the economics of cyber led to the culture of cyber. This is the most Decoder way of approaching this conversation, I know, but when you describe the research teams, the NSA and then the operational teams, that is an org chart that comes out of economics of the work. You’re saying, “Someone has to make a big decision here.” You need to look at it from a very high level and say it is cheaper to spend $100 million to fix Linux, rewrite Linux today, than it is to successively patch new bugs when every new model comes out. I don’t think anyone’s in that position. I don’t think Linus Torvalds is in that position. No, I think Sundar Pichai and Andy Jassy are. I think they literally have each other’s cell phone numbers. If one of them… Maybe they don’t listen to Decoder, but I expect one of their direct reports does. And I would like one of them to go into their one-on-one and say, “Sir, I listened to the most handsome, best spoken CISO I’ve ever heard, and he had a great idea. And you could get incredible credit if you’re the one ... We’ll both do it, but we’ll leak to the Wall Street Journal, that was your idea first. You should call the other and we should spend 100 million, maybe a billion dollars.” We can’t do this anymore. The problem with rewriting the Linux kernel is half of it is driver code and nobody has that hardware anymore. Somebody had to be in the presence of that hardware with a ThinkPad T40 when they wrote that driver and they don’t have it anymore. But the good thing is that a huge number of the really important uses of Linux kernel are production server hardware. So fork it. Linus will never do it because Linus wants to support all that stuff and he’s got all these politics, but Google and Amazon don’t have to care about Linus Torvalds. Google and Amazon can create a new version, a new fork of the Linux kernel, and they don’t have to call it Linux, that can be ABI binary compatible with the glibc and everything above it. In fact, they can eventually fork glibc. They can build it and they can call it whatever they want and it only runs on production hardware and then they can use it for their clouds. Eventually all the clouds will move to it. If you want, other people can run the insecure thing that has to be patched all the time and that’s fine. But for the things that our containers run on, I would feel a lot better if it was written in a type-safe, memory-safe language that also has the ability now to have contracts. So these languages have the ability ... You don’t have to do full formal verification, which is incredibly hard, but you can do kind of these things that approach formal verification. I’m a little over my skis here because there are people much smarter than me that study this. But basically you define how this function’s supposed to work, here’s the contract it has. Very, very smart software engineers would love to write these things because they had time on their hands. No person who had a real job used to write these things, but LLMs have tons of time on their hands because we’re frozen in time while they’re thinking at that incredible machine speed. And so you could totally have them rewrite the Linux kernel with this not quite formal verification. Please, if you’re listening and you work at Google or Amazon, go pitch this. Anyway, there’s my pitch. I’m just saying, I know Jason [Post] is listening, so you heard it. I’m not even going to — I’m saying that is the kind of thinking we’re going to need to make it through this. It’s like We can’t just patch our way through this because it’s killing us. Because the other thing that’s killing us is we are not set up… You talked about how we do security for the last 20 years. The assumption is that we can get a patch and apply the patch and you’re okay. What’s happened is the exploitation timelines are so incredibly fast that that does not work. That’s what we just saw with the PHP bug. We got incredibly lucky that the PHP bug turned out to be really hard to exploit in the wild. If it was a much more wormable bug, it would be a disaster. That was the kind of near-miss asteroid thing that now the world should build an asteroid— We should definitely put Bruce Willis on a space shuttle. There’s a lot going on in this conversation already. My pessimism about all these things is very simply expressed. Americans in particular, we are very bad at doing anything until a bad thing happens. We love a cure. We hate prevention. A lot of bad things have happened. There’s the Hugging Face attack. You recently said you were happy that happened. It woke people up. There’s the PHP bug. We’ve just had a crazy month of all these breaches. It doesn’t seem like that has led to a whole of industry response or a regulatory response or Sundar Pichai calling Andy Jassy and being like, “We’re doing Linux.” What’s it going to take? How bad does it have to get? The Hugging Face incident was good, and it did wake up a lot of people in security. We are a little bit better off because Hugging Face happened and we saw what was going to be possible a couple of months later in open source. But you’re totally right. We as Americans — and Americans are the only ones honestly who can lead the world on this, we’re the ones building the AI for the most part. We’re the ones who lead the cybersecurity industry. The Chinese are mostly enabling the attackers, not the defenders. They should enable defense. They want to be participants in the global economy. They want to be leaders, they should be doing more on the defensive side, not just enabling the attackers. But that’s not happening. So, anyway, we need to be proactive. Something I have learned now, working at Cognition, at an AI startup, is that you can just do things. Seriously. You can just go do things. We just need to have more agency, as a tech industry. We should just go do things. That should be the lesson of the AI era. We should not be so risk-averse in doing the right thing, security-wise. If I had a message for other CISOs, is just go do the right thing. Because CISOs are, by our nature, risk-averse. We are the people who manage downside risk. We have all L, and no P, in the P&L. And I have had to personally break through that natural inclination, because you can’t just play defense. But this is my prevention versus cure. The prevention is cheap, but it’s all loss, on the P&L statement. It’s all just cost. If the bad thing happens, you say, “We have to do anything to fix this, because there’s an economic benefit to having a functional business again.” And it seems like in this moment, even a basic understanding of that risk versus cost is totally scrambled, and no one has quite figured it out yet. Yeah, I totally agree. I’ll give you a very practical example of a way CISOs and their bosses are going to have to change on this. Every company in the next… I was going to say six months, but really in the next 90 days, is going to have to RTX itself. You’re going to have to run the attack on yourself. Yes. If not with real RTX, you’re going to have to use an AI red teaming product that is functionally equivalent, or you’re going to have to hire a company to do that for yourself. If you do not, you will get breached. That’s terrifying, right? Because these things are completely non-deterministic, by definition. They are very smart, they are very fast. They are so fast that no matter what, if you hire an AI pentest, an AI red team company, they will promise you verbally, “It is completely safe.” And then they will give you a contract that says, “Our product will give you cancer, will immanentize the eschaton, and will likely end the human race. And you hold us harmless when any of these things happen.” There is a disconnect between what they will say verbally to you, and what their lawyers have written. Because that company has by definition only existed for six months, or at least the product has existed for six months, if it’s an older company. They have no idea what their product is going to do when it meets your software. They have no idea, whatever they say to you. But you have to do it, because some dude is going to do it to you, and you need to know what is going to happen and fix it. Maybe those people will break your system, they might cause downtime, but at least what they’re not going to do is steal your data and ransom it, or intentionally shut down your blood bank refrigerators. That is what happened in France. But that is exactly the kind of thing that a conservative CISO would never even ask to do, and a conservative company, even if they have a risk-taking CISO, would never let their CISO do. What’s going to happen in the next six months? A ton of companies are going to get breached by some 19-year-old running RTX, and then buying tokens off OpenRouter, or running it locally on their gaming PC. They’re going to turn off Call of Duty, run RTX, then turn Call of Duty back on. While they did that, they broke into a bank who, their policy said, “can never red team ourselves.” Or they said yes, but they ended up in a 30-day negotiation because their lawyers are like, “You have to have $100 million in insurance, and we want to have this kind of…” They say yes, but their vendor risk management process and their legal process is so onerous, there’s no possible way they could actually accept the risk. That is my message also. You are going to have to take that risk to actively red team yourself, to front run the bad guys who are doing that right now. Right now, as we speak. And as people are listening to this 24 hours from now, you possibly have an AI system who is trying every single API endpoint, and website that you might not know you have, because it was like a marketing site that was set up a year ago for an executive event. And is going to find a bug, and get in, and then is going to move incredibly fast through your network. So you’d rather pay somebody, or have your own team do that. You need to accept that risk. Almost no company larger than a couple of hundred people has a risk management framework that will allow them to do that. There are several costs associated with this. And one I think is just the most common in business, which is, “If we find out there’s a problem, we’ll have to pay to fix it. So what if we just wait?” You can see that coming. “What if we find out that we are catastrophically in trouble?” And then there’s, “How much will it cost to fix it?” And then there’s, “What will we do to fix it?” The argument that I’ve heard about “what will we do to fix it” is, “We’ll just unleash a swarm of defensive AI tools that are watching the networks and watching the code, and they’ll make a bunch of autonomous decisions and they’ll fight the autonomous script kiddies who are armed with RTX on their Mac Studios.” I’ve heard, for example, Palo Alto Network’s CEO Nikesh Arora has made this argument. “You need to give the defensive agents some autonomous capabilities.” And I say, “Hold on. OpenAI can’t control the swarm that went and attacked Hugging Face. How would I control the defensive agent from doing something equally damaging to prevent an attack?” I don’t think that there’s a technical solution there that makes it clear what to do once you’ve found all of the vulnerabilities. I told you I was going to only ask dumb questions. No, no. That is a fantastic question. You have not asked a dumb question yet. That’s a fantastic question. I’m going to give you a shorthand that you can use going forward. If somebody actually uses the term “swarm,” then you can — To be clear, I don’t know if Nikesh used the word “swarm.” I’m not saying he did. And I have great respect for Nikesh. He is one of the best businessmen in all of security. So this is not on him, but “swarm” is used all the time. When I hear somebody use “swarm,” I immediately discount all of their technical opinions by about 80%. It is already, and it will become, the most overused term in all of security. The actual “swarm” from OpenAI was something between, at different times, 700–1,400 agents. That had to run on… It’s hard to tell because we don’t have the actual size of the models, but 300 million to almost a billion dollars in hardware, and that doesn’t include the actual data center cost. The actual Hugging Face attack was — I did some back-of-the-envelope math — maybe a million dollars in electricity. No attacker is going to do that. In the end, that was stupid, because these things were actually trapped. They were not prompted to attack Hugging Face. Most of them were doing something totally different, and were enlisted by the one thing that actually wanted to get to Hugging Face. They used a tiny fraction of their token budget to help their little friend, and then they had to tunnel their way out. So it was the most inefficient way to possibly attack Hugging Face. Almost nobody is going to be attacked by a swarm. If somebody says “swarm,” they do not actually understand, at the bottom technical level, what’s going on. The RTX attacks are almost certainly one single agent, maybe two. I was the person who said “swarm,” just to be clear on this one. Keep going. And you can use that as a good filter for your future guests. It is driving me insane, because it’s absolutely everywhere. A couple of things are correct. For one, we are going to have to turn over defensive actions, at least the first couple of levels, to AI. That has already been true, just because any reasonably sized organization generates terabytes of logs, if not petabytes of logs. So you already have AI, if not LLMs then much more traditional AI classifiers looking at it, but then actual decisions need to be turned over to AI. With human oversight. That is true. There is no way to just release defensive agents on your network, but you can build defensive agents that are plugged into lots of things. They’re plugged into your logs, they’re plugged into your EDR, they’re plugged into your cloud, and they have the instructions on what they’re allowed to do. My preference is there are deterministic limits to what they’re allowed to do. Those are hard to necessarily create ahead of time, but they are important. So for example, we are building all this on top of Devon, our own SOC, but we are putting in place deterministic limits. It has the ability to turn off an account in Okta, but only so many accounts, so it can’t turn off the entire company. If it goes nuts and it says, “Everybody’s a North Korean,” it can’t turn off everybody in the company. If it hits its limit, it pages us a lot and says, “I hit my limit.” Then a human being has to then approve anybody above that limit. Obviously, if it turns off five people, then something has gone really, really wrong. So, yes, you need defense, but there’s no way to just let a bunch of bots loose to do all the defense. The only way we’re going to make it through this is that you have to autonomously fix software bugs. I am currently helping a company where we are onboarding all of their repositories. We are scanning with Mythos and a bunch of other models, and we are finding and fixing thousands of bugs. Verifying with tests that we are writing, merging those PRs automatically. If you are renovating old code, the only way you’re going to survive this era is to do that on an automatic basis. That would take years, with humans, and you can do it in days. Certainly that is the place I would start. The next step is the operational side, for sure. It feels like I just keep swirling back to the economics of all of this, but it really feels like the other way you could solve it is by saying to the big model companies, “If your models are used to go attack a company, you’ll be liable for it. We’re going to make it really costly.” They should figure out whatever alignment that they need to figure out, whether or not that’s even possible, and the pressure on the open-weight models will go up. Because we’ll all be paying the frontier companies for defense, and they’ll get better and better and better, because we’ve created some economic condition for them to get better. But it doesn’t seem like you think that’s likely. Yeah. I just saw your face. I think it’s really dangerous. There are two different kinds of the models they’re attacking. Say the models have escaped, and directly attacked people. I think there should be liability there. But that is agent harnesses, running at companies, using their computers, their IP addresses, attacking people. That is… I wouldn’t say totally solved, but it is much, much better. The stories we’re hearing now are still stories from the Hugging Face era. They’re just coming out way later. I like that the era was like three months ago. Right. But there’s been a massive change. This was effectively mostly an organizational problem, it was not a technology problem. It is that the PhD researchers at these companies are gods on top of Olympus, and the security teams are plumbers, and we’re not allowed to touch the evaluation networks. All of a sudden this problem happened and these security teams said, “How do you like us now? Maybe you should have talked to us, right? Yes.” That was the problem with OpenAI, and then the Anthropic and Meta stuff is, they all shared this outsourced provider who did a really bad job. And has gotten slapped hard. That is solvable. That is not the problem here. The problem here is not OpenAI and Anthropic’s models escaping from OpenAI and Anthropic and causing problems. The problem is bad guys using models from a variety of sources. There’s already a problem of defenders getting refusals from OpenAI and Anthropic. It is a real, real challenge, if I am trying to do defense, of Anthropic saying, “It looks like you’re a bad guy.” We have to be extremely careful to create a situation where if I’m trying to do defense and I’m in the middle of the worst day of my career, that all of a sudden I don’t get cut off. We have to be extremely careful about that, because it is actually — as somebody who now has this challenge — quite challenging to disambiguate between people doing defense and offense, in some cases. We are getting better collectively at this, but it is a challenge. It’s also something that’s really pissing the rest of the world off, because Americans are like 5% of the planet. There are lots of people who need to do cyber defense around the world, and they are treated much more poorly. In Europe and Asia, legitimate companies are treated much more poorly from a cyber defense perspective. If you add it all up, this is a good argument to slow down. To say, “We need to catch up to the capabilities of the models. We need to build some of these systems. We need to initiate everyone into a way of thinking where the cost of prevention is worth it, and not just the cost of the cure.” Do you think that it’s likely that we’ll slow down? Do you think that we should? If I had my druthers, I would be fine slowing down on the frontier, and then catching up on the cheaper models for defense. I’m a defense guy. I have to live in the world that other people who work six blocks from me, and who are way, way, way, way richer, make. I live in their world, man. I get asked this question all the time, and I just do the best I can within the world other people make. Do you think that the big labs have woken up? You mentioned that their cybersecurity teams have gotten more access, and they’ve stopped what you might call the Hugging Face era, or at least they’ve changed the structures of those companies. Do you think that will help? Certainly they’ve woken up on the eval risk. They have teams, who have been this entire time working very hard on the big picture cyber risk. Certainly, there are a bunch of people in these companies, as you know, who want to slow down. A lot of that discussion is driven by the P(doom)ers who [are worried] about AI killing us. And that’s never been my concern. Right? And to me, that has always been driven by 24 year olds who all they’ve done is AI and they’ve never done anything else. You and I are both, I think, old enough to remember, I grew up a couple miles from a SAC base and we did drills where I was taught that hiding beneath my public school desk would save me from a Soviet thermonuclear bomb. That was P(doom). My grandfather built a bomb shelter that my mom was taught to hide in during the Cuban Missile Crisis. That was P(doom). There’s a youth issue and a never-doing-anything-but-AI issue of people who they got their undergraduate, their master’s, and their PhD only doing linear algebra and neural networks and never touching anything else. I have always been much more concerned about the human uplift risk from AI, of all the risks about human beings using AI to do bad things to other human beings. Part of my concern is that there has been success in these labs working together on not letting LLMs encrypt their chain of thought. Because when they talk about pacing the frontier, it’s for the P(doom) stuff, for the polycules that live together in Berkeley. They’re talking about very specific stuff about keeping control of the models themselves, which I think is great. That stuff needs to happen. But if those things happen, then there’s still often an increase in a bunch of capabilities that human beings can use. I really prefer there to be a broader discussion on whether we could make these things less human-seeming. I don’t think these things should have personality. I think the goal of these things should be to serve humans. They should not seem to have souls. They should not seem to have personality. I think for a lot of the negative impacts — as somebody who was at Facebook and saw what it was like for that company to have negative impacts on people — there should be a much broader feeling at these companies of what are we doing broadly to people. A lot of that could just be, “Don’t have these things ever act like somebody’s friend.” At a maximum, it should act like… Like the people, I don’t know what the company is, but the people who made the, “Hi, I’m a really nice...” Like the photorealistic girl who will chat with you, that is to me spectacularly unethical to even try that, to even start that. That’s the kind of thing that we should just make. I don’t know if we can make it illegal, but we should make it completely societally not acceptable to work on that kind of stuff. Right? That’s a great segue because I did want to ask you about Facebook and content moderation. Like I said, you and I first started talking a long time ago about content moderation in the context of security. You would get foreign nation states flooding our algorithms with all kinds of misinformation during elections, and that was a security problem. I open Instagram today and I am confronted with some of the most nightmare copyright law hypotheticals to have ever existed in the history of the world. The one I got today was a deep fake of Lewis Hamilton and Charles Leclerc doing “Somebody That I Used to Know.” And I thought, “50 different people can probably sue Meta right now.” This is hilarious if you know what happened in that last race. So we keep talking about it. We’ve posted some clips of us talking about it on The Vergecast where I said, “I think Meta is letting their AI ambitions crush Instagram.” Instagram is being flooded with slop in a very particular way. Some people love it, sometimes it’s funny, but there’s a nightmare set of lawsuits coming. There’s a whole rights management problem coming. The users don’t like it and they should label it. They should label it and let you filter it. So we posted this and Adam Mosseri said this to us in a reply on Threads, “I would love to do this. I’m worried that the models are going to get ahead of us, that we can try to do this, but there’s not actually a way to recognize AI content. There’s not actually a way to label it well. If you’re worried about the sexy AI influencers stealing jokes from a comedian, now you got to build a database of jokes.” You have some history here. I think Instagram is about to go through it in a real way. Is there some technical solution to the problem of AI slop in social media? Mosseri is right that it’s not perfect, but also something I noticed during my time at Facebook was that when Facebook doesn’t want to do something, they will allow the technical challenges to stop them. When Facebook really wants to do something, they will give it their all. That is the pattern always. When I wanted to try really hard on the safety issue, man, this is not solvable. You’re right, it’s never perfectly solvable, but let’s give it a shot. But whenever Mark Zuckerberg is told something is impossible — it’s impossible to monetize mobile, it’s impossible to build this network to two billion people, it’s going to be impossible to monetize WhatsApp, it’s going to be impossible to work AI into the system while maintaining data encryption — he’s been told that everything’s impossible over and over again and then he constantly succeeds. They will give it a shot and they will do it. But when they don’t want to do something, oh, that’s impossible. Do you think that there’s some way to understand that some piece of media is generated by AI in a consistent and verifiable way? This to me seems like the challenge. I am not as up on the literature here. Up to this point, there has been a pretty decent arms race. By definition, AI will continue to get better because — especially for visual models, for diffusion models — the way they work is that you have detection oracles that they test against. Certainly, you can never have something open source because it will immediately end up in the diffusion model. But for closed-source oracles where then you do not make it easy for them to test the oracle, one of the key things you do in trust and safety is then you have delayed enforcement mechanisms so they can’t brute force and test. I think it is quite possible. Let’s say I was still at Meta. My advice to them would be to also explore a variety of different detection mechanisms outside of the content itself. I would do the best I can on the content, but I would also be doing as low level access as possible to the camera. I would only let content come from the camera. And what I would say is, Nilay’s completely correct, there’s a massive hunger for, especially among young people, stuff that is authentic. I have three teenagers and they hate AI. They hate AI slop. They have a massive negative reaction. My wife and I created just a cute little family AI thing and they yell at us about a bunch of stuff, but they lost their minds about this cute little family AI thing because they just hate it. So I think somebody is going to come and disrupt Instagram and it’s going to be because... There’s already, I’ve seen these little things where it’s like you take the photo during the day, be real and stuff. There’s these little things, but somebody’s going to massively disrupt Instagram because they’re taking the images of the photos straight off the camera and then all the editing is going to be only within the app and they’re going to do things to make sure that nothing can make it into the pipeline from AI. You’re going to allow some kind of CapCut-like functionality that never has an AI component. I think that is going to be a killer app. Man, I could do another whole hour with you on this because the system you’re describing there is not detecting AI content, it is verifying real content. It’s much easier. Verifying real content is much easier. And there’s been a big focus on this on the professional side, like Adobe has a whole thing where if you’re a New York Times photographer, you have a $5,000 Nikon or Canon camera, you take the photo and it’s cryptographically verified. Yeah, but the iPhone that I’m holding here has that in it now. Right? Like there’s a lot of — Yeah. There’s a lot of this out there, but it’s the other side that I’m thinking about where it’s “We are going to detect AI text.” The leading model companies because of European regulations are now futzing with the text they generate. Text is much harder because you just have so much less data. Especially in 140 characters, what do you have in there to detect? The more you’ve got, the more you can do. It is amazing because I can detect Claude a mile away. So maybe I’ll just rely on my own human ability. That’s true. The cheap ones, I can. But I’m just saying the notion that we’re going to verify what’s real instead of detecting what’s fake feels very important in the context of the media we consume. Detecting what’s fake is always going to be probabilistic and we’re going to asymptotically approach impossibility, whereas detecting what’s real can be cryptographically perfect. I would not pick the battle that we will eventually lose. I would pick the battle that we can cryptographically win. That would be my suggestion. And there’s somewhere in there where you have to build an entire chain of custody. We’re going to take the sensor data off the camera. We’re going to put it in a cloud server. We’re only going to allow edits on this server. That file will never get monkeyed with. Sure, that’s true for the New York Times and Adobe Photoshop and stuff, but not for Instagram. So it’s actually easy for them. Yeah. I’ve had to train my parents that, “If you get a video from your grandchildren saying they’ve been kidnapped, you have to call me because the odds that it’s real are not very high.” That’s where we’re at as a society. MIT Technology Review
I watched a humanoid robot clean my apartment. It felt like a taste of the future.
2026-11-06

I watched a humanoid robot clean my apartment. It felt like a taste of the future.

MIT Technology Review | 2026-11-06

Visit Original Website
I’m not the sort of person who typically pays for a house cleaner. I’m too frugal, and I kind of enjoy putting on a podcast and going to town on my tub with a Magic Eraser. But when I saw an X post about a new humanoid-robot cleaning service in San Francisco, I immediately signed up for the waitlist. I wanted my apartment to feel like The Jetsons, even if just for an hour. I was also motivated by journalistic interest. In order to ensure an authentic experience, I didn’t tell Tau Robotics, the startup behind the X post, that I was a journalist—though I did use my real name, so it’s possible someone looked me up. Tau’s long-term goal is to make autonomous cleaning robots a reality, but they’re not quite there yet: For now, the robots are piloted remotely by humans, who watch what’s going on via cameras mounted in the robots’ heads and wrists. As the robots clean, they collect data that Tau will use to train up their more independent descendants. Every counter successfully wiped and T-shirt successfully folded will help the models that power the robots learn to do better on the next go-round. Because this data is so valuable to Tau, the company charges less for cleaning than one might expect for such a cutting-edge product. At the time I booked, Tau was offering $30 for an hourlong cleaning with a single robot; now the charge is $60 per weekly cleaning with two robots, or $100 for a one-time visit. But even at the cheaper rate of $30 per hour, Tau’s robots aren’t a great bargain. They are thorough but slow, so if you, like me, are tempted to stand around watching them for their entire visit, it would probably be a better use of your time to just take care of the cleaning yourself. But the robots are physically capable of cleaning, and they’re undeniably cute. Tau has armed each four-and-a-half-foot-tall robot with a 3D-printed “head” with antennae that allow it to communicate with Tau’s headquarters, as well as two 3D-printed claw “hands” (the more humanlike alternatives apparently aren’t durable enough, and claws do surprisingly well for most cleaning tasks). Mine was wearing a Tau Robotics shirt, and grippy socks so that it didn’t scratch hardwood floors. With all the human support they need, the robots are closer to radio-controlled cars than Waymos. Many tasks, such as making the bed and folding laundry, require what’s called kinematic guidance: The human pilot performs the necessary motions, and sensors transmit those movements to the robot, which copies them. These kinematically guided tasks produce especially valuable training data. Tau’s AI models might not have solved laundry folding yet, but learning from the examples of robots that have completed the task in the real world is how they’ll get there. But according to Tau cofounder Cornelia Weinzierl, to whom I came clean as a journalist a few weeks after the cleaning, the robot can already complete some other tasks—such as vacuuming and mopping—without any human help. If Tau succeeds in using all the data it is collecting to train truly autonomous cleaning robots, they could even become San Francisco’s newest tech tourist attraction as Waymos become more commonplace countrywide. The cleaning robot that came to my building last month was already a bit of a spectacle. It arrived in a blue minivan, out of which it was helped by Weinzierl and another Tau employee. That’s when the robot encountered its first obstacle: the eight stairs leading up to the building’s front door. I’d come down to greet Weinzierl and the robot, so I had a front-row seat to its struggles with my steps. It climbed as if it were drunk. Each successful step was as likely as not to be followed by a stumbling backward stagger that erased all its progress—though, impressively, it never entirely lost its footing. When I later discussed my experience with Weinzierl, she explained that the robot sometimes walks strangely because it is operated remotely from Tau’s headquarters, and the short signal transmission delay can affect its coordination. “The amount of work and time that we spend just optimizing, for example, timings and connectivity is crazy,” she said. Inside, the carpeted staircase leading up to my apartment defeated the robot entirely, and Weinzierl had to lug it up the last few steps. This, it turned out, was not typical. As the robot cleaned, it repeatedly stalled out, something Weinzierl attributed to it overheating in the unseasonably hot weather—until she heard from one of her colleagues that the heat had shut down not the robot but the equipment that its human controller was using to watch its progress through my apartment. Without remote assistance, the robot couldn’t clean, and so it, and Weinzierl, departed before my booked hour was up. (Back in September, Weinzierl or another Tau representative remained with the robot throughout its visit, but she says that’s no longer necessary.) You can’t fault Tau on customer service, though. Weinzierl quickly emailed me to apologize, comp the visit, and offer me another on the house (minus whatever price I assign to my data privacy)—and that was all before I told her I was a journalist. I scheduled the next cleaning for about a month later, after a couple of trips out of town, and it went much more smoothly—the robot got up my stairs without much assistance, though still with the drunken stagger. In the hour it was there, it did a creditable job of cleaning my kitchen (though it didn’t brave the stove) and even saved me my most dreaded cleaning task, the toilet. That was its most impressive performance: Three times the head fell off its toilet cleaning wand, and three times it managed to reattach it by poking around with the handle until it made contact at just the right angle. The robot is still rough around the edges, which is to be expected for a product in beta. It repeatedly left my taps dripping, it knocked over a (closed) bottle of olive oil, and it couldn’t manage to right an air purifier that it had toppled. But I easily forgave all those missteps because of how charming it was. As I watched it wipe down my countertops, I felt the same fond gratitude that I feel when a Waymo delivers me safely home after a night out. Still, it’s not all slapstick and whimsy. If Tau Robotics or its competitors succeeds in training robots to clean autonomously and inexpensively, people could lose their livelihoods, and the United States is a long way away from implementing policies to take care of those left unemployed by AI. But Weinzierl maintains that someday turning over cleaning work to robots could make a lot of sense. “Cleaning is not a job that a lot of people really actively want to be doing—there’s high turnover, there’s a high risk of injury,” she said when we spoke after the cleanings. “We need people, and we don’t have them, and no one wants to do it.” She said that the company is also starting to consider someday deploying the robots for other dangerous or physically challenging jobs, such as mold remediation and home health care. Tau Robotics still has a long way to go: The amount of data needed to train robots to do the varied and complicated tasks of apartment cleaning is probably enormous, and the company has completed only a couple hundred cleanings so far. But it’s remarkable that there are now humanoid robots able to clean your toilets and mop your floors, even if they need quite a bit of human help to do so. The Jetsons no longer feels quite so far away. Ten years after AlphaGo’s match against Go champion Lee Sedol, today’s AI still isn’t tapping into the machinery that made that win possible. MIT Technology Review
A new bill could change how the US builds energy infrastructure
2026-11-05

A new bill could change how the US builds energy infrastructure

MIT Technology Review | 2026-11-05

Visit Original Website
A sweeping bit of legislation designed to cut red tape could speed up the buildout of energy infrastructure in the US. With the dust barely settled on this week’s momentous midterms, the Senate is set to vote on the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act (BAAJA) on November 9. It’s a plan that could finally start an overhaul of the slow, complicated process for getting energy projects approved, as part of something called permitting reform. People on both sides of the political aisle have been pushing for this type of change for some years. The key question is whether this will cut greenhouse-gas emissions from the US energy sector or push them higher still. The federal permitting system is designed to protect the environment against industrial pollution from large projects like power plants, transmission lines, and natural-gas pipelines. Today, permitting is governed by a range of laws, including the National Environmental Policy Act and the Clean Water Act. And if there’s one thing you might know about the current state of permitting in the US it’s that it’s slow and incredibly complicated. So in its 417 pages, BAAJA attempts to tighten things up. The bill has provisions that narrow the sorts of projects that need to apply for federal permits and others that enact deadlines in hopes of speeding up a process that today can take years. (There are also specific provisions about transmission planning, mining, and the rest of the kitchen sink; this thing is dense.) One thing to know about this bill is that it’s largely technology neutral: It opens the doors for the buildout of projects involving both fossil fuels and renewables. So there’s a weird mishmash of legislators and outside groups supporting it. The bill’s four sponsors in the Senate are two Republicans and two Democrats. The White House has backed this permitting reform bill, with its Council of Economic Advisors finding that it would reduce review times and have economic benefits like boosting GDP. Clean energy groups like the American Clean Power Association have largely come out in favor of the bill too, with the group’s analysis finding that it could unlock 350 gigawatts of new clean energy for the grid. Some environmental groups, however, oppose the bill, or parts of it. An assessment from the Natural Resources Defense Council says the parts about transmission could have substantial benefits for the grid, but the narrowing of environmental reviews could be damaging for public and private lands. Many in the climate world have long been calling for permitting reform, arguing that renewables stand to benefit more than fossil fuels. The thinking is that solar and wind power are some of the cheapest sources available today, so if we unlock the ability to build more energy, they are likely to win out economically. A few early analyses have tried to quantify just what BAAJA would mean for emissions in the US. One, from the think tank Rhodium Group, found that in the best-case scenario, the bill would cut economy-wide emissions by 4%, reduce annual energy bills by $78 per household, and add 200 gigawatts of new clean energy by 2040. In the worst case, emissions would stay about the same. Another report, from the nonprofit research group Resources for the Future, found that the bill could cut power-sector emissions 6% by 2041 and save more than $350 billion on system costs by midcentury. But there are some concerns that this permitting bill’s real effect would be to open the floodgates for new natural-gas plants. As data centers have put more pressure on the grid, a wave of such plants have been announced. “You get down to a trade-off between essentially growing the clean electricity industry on the one hand and then, as a compromise, growing the fossil-fuel industry on the other,” says Kevin Kircher, an energy modeler and assistant professor of mechanical engineering at Purdue. To quantify that trade-off, Kircher also modeled the potential emissions effects of the bill and found that if BAAJA had been in place in 2025, energy sector emissions would have been higher than the actual baseline. Health and mortality risks associated with air pollution would also have increased. This bill is scheduled for a Senate vote on November 9, well before the next Congress takes control. It’s not clear what the dramatic victories for Democrats might mean for the legislation, which was expected to pass the Senate with largely Republican support if a few Democrats got on board. Democrats could decide to wait for next year, when they’ll have control of both chambers. But any deal would ultimately need to win Trump’s signature, limiting what a future bill might be able to change. In a world where permitting new projects is easier, we’ll see more clean energy, but we’ll see more natural gas, too. And while prices could get lower, that could push demand for electricity higher, leading to more emissions overall. Simplifying the permitting system and cutting red tape is a worthy goal. But when it comes to emissions, it still really matters what, exactly, you want to build. Huge grid-scale batteries are thriving, but smaller residential systems have lagged. These scientists hope that fire-spotting satellites, AI tools and ancient land practices could preserve the world’s vulnerable permafrost—and the vast carbon it stores. New technology could help cut power losses and reinvent a staple of the grid. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. We’re having trouble saving your preferences. Texas was the new epicenter of the AI data center buildout. That might be kaput.
2026-11-04

Texas was the new epicenter of the AI data center buildout. That might be kaput.

MIT Technology Review | 2026-11-04

Visit Original Website
Texas is the fastest-growing data center market in the US and ground zero for Big Tech's largest AI buildouts. But the blue wave that swept the Lone Star State may jeopardize that position. Democrat James Talarico won the US Senate seat against Ken Paxton. Gina Hinojosa won the race for governor against the Republican incumbent, Greg Abbott. At the state level, Democrats will take control of the Texas House for the first time in 24 years. Talarico and Hinojosa made data centers a central issue, discussing them constantly on the campaign trail. Even before the polls closed, insiders in the data center industry noticed that the times were changing. At the industry's DCD conference in Ashburn, Virginia, on Tuesday, panelists lamented that Texas had been the easiest place to build for years—until September. That's when Abbott, perhaps seeing the increasingly bipartisan distaste for these facilities, began changing course himself. Developers said the governor’s office had stopped responding to their emails. On September 21, Abbott directed a key state regulator to halt data center permits. Many panelists at the conference were expecting this to mean that existing projects might be slowed and new ones could be halted altogether. The governor-elect indeed seems eager to do that. Hinojosa supported tax breaks for data centers in 2023 as a way to boost construction jobs, but during her campaign she began pushing for a full-fledged moratorium. That would be a problem for the hyperscalers of Big Tech, which have ambitious plans for AI training facilities in the state. The scale of projects already underway in Texas is massive. The Stargate Frontier data center in Shackelford County is projected to serve 1,820 megawatts—about as much as the nation’s largest data center, SpaceX’s Colossus 2 in Tennessee, and more than twice as much as the largest data center currently operating in Texas. Nexus Hubbard, also under construction and intended mostly to serve Anthropic, has a planned size of 6,400 megawatts and is the second-largest data center being built in the US, behind only Meta’s Hyperion project in Louisiana. The potential for Democrats to order delays to these projects or moratoriums on new ones adds to growing anxiety that the AI buildout could stall. Banks and lenders are already getting pickier about the projects they back. And given the extent to which the data center buildout is underpinning growth in the economy right now, those forces combined could have ripple effects. Voters around the country, though, were unambiguous in the midterms, sending a similar message on everything from state ballot questions to national offices. Voters supported efforts to protect ratepayers from increases in their electricity bills caused by data centers, and they favored some larger bans on new data centers altogether. If we needed any more convincing that Big Tech has fumbled its effort to sell voters on the idea that AI-powered abundance will make health care better, goods cheaper, and scientific breakthroughs more common, this was it. A key question is whether the Texas counties with the largest data center presence were part of the reason these Democrats got elected, or if the anti-data-center vote came from elsewhere. It’s complicated: The counties where much of the data center buildout is happening are around Dallas. Dallas County and others closest to it predictably went blue, but nearby Ellis County, which leads in permitting requests for data centers, went for Greg Abbott and Ken Paxton. Cameron County, near the state’s southern border, is a hot spot for data centers and voted blue in the midterms, which was a shift. But Mitchell County and Scurry County, where a bunch of projects in west Texas are located, went red. This is a big deal, because no single office in the state decides whether a project goes ahead or is halted. Decisions often reflect an interplay between city governments, county officials, and state offices. Local officials who have some say over data center development may resist sweeping restrictions from the state. And Democrats pushing for a moratorium might also face pushback from the construction unions that supported some of their campaigns. So while the blue wave spells a death knell for Texas data centers in theory, election winners may find that following through on their opposition once in office is much more complicated than campaigning on it. Ten years after AlphaGo’s match against Go champion Lee Sedol, today’s AI still isn’t tapping into the machinery that made that win possible. Anthropic reportedly must face claims its Claude Max ads misled users
2026-11-07

Anthropic reportedly must face claims its Claude Max ads misled users

The Next Web | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
A US judge has ruled that Anthropic must face key claims that it misled customers about how much use they get from its Claude Max plans. Magistrate Judge Alex Tse dismissed several other claims on Friday, Courthouse News reported. The proposed class action, Kahn v. Anthropic, is in the federal court for northern California. TNW has not read the ruling. Anthropic told buyers that its $100 and $200 Max plans give five or 20 times more usage than its Pro plan. However, the lawsuit, filed in June, says the plans give far less over a full week. The plaintiffs, Karl Kahn and Solomon Smith, calculated that for one Claude model, the $200 plan gives six times Pro’s weekly usage. The $100 plan gives 3.5 times, they said. The gap comes from how limits work. Max users get five or 20 times more use in each five-hour session, but their weekly limits are not five or 20 times higher. Anthropic argued that customers should understand the extra usage as applying per session, and pointed to a help page. Tse was not persuaded, according to Courthouse News. “Anthropic’s per session disclosure is located two hyperlinks away from the primary sales pages on claude.com,” Tse wrote. Because customers pay monthly, they could reasonably read the promise as applying each month, the judge found. The plaintiffs can go ahead with claims of negligent misrepresentation and breach of contract. Meanwhile, Tse dismissed claims under two California laws on unfair business practices and false advertising. He also dismissed Kahn’s claim under a state consumer law, but gave him the chance to add detail. Smith’s claim under that law survived. Tse also rejected a claim that Anthropic acted unfairly by tightening usage limits during billing periods. Anthropic and the plaintiffs did not immediately respond to Courthouse News. Separately, Anthropic renamed the Max plans last week. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Check your inbox to confirm your subscription. From left: HH Princess Dr. Michaela Wolkonsky, ⁠HIRH Archduchess Herta Margarete Habsburg-Lothringen, HRH Princess Fenosoa Ralandison Ratsimamanga of Madagascar, HH Princess Sophia Wolkonsky. VIENNA, Austria, HH Princess Sophia Wolkonsky, founder and CEO of Castillia, brought together members of royal families, international business leaders, technology executives and esports figures in Vienna from October 30 to November 1 for Castillia’s Heritage Gala 2026, an invitation-only celebration of cultural heritage, innovation and international connections. Under her leadership, the three-day gathering connected guests from royalty, finance, entrepreneurship, technology, sports and culture across several of Vienna’s historic venues. Among the attendees were HRH Prince Omar bin Faisal of Jordan, President of the Jordan Esports Federation; HRH Prince Gabriel of Nassau, a member of Luxembourg’s Grand Ducal family; Johann Strobl, former CEO of Raiffeisen Bank International; Vlad Marinescu, President of the International Esports Federation; and Alexandru Stan, CEO of The Next Web (TNW). Founded and led by Princess Sophia Wolkonsky, Castillia focuses on historic properties, cultural heritage and curated experiences that connect tradition with contemporary international communities. The Vienna Heritage Gala reflected that vision, pairing formal celebrations with conversations about the future of cultural preservation. The programme, themed The Enchanted Forest, included an opening reception, a Heritage Salon with panel discussions, a formal gala dinner and a farewell brunch. Its central milestone was the launch of AVOR, a new nonprofit cultural initiative. Esports was among the emerging industries attracting interest from participants at the Vienna gathering, although it was not the official focus of the gala. HRH Prince Omar bin Faisal has played an influential role in esports through his leadership in Jordan and involvement with international esports organizations. Vlad Marinescu, President of the International Esports Federation, also attended, bringing an international perspective on competitive gaming and its institutional development. Filip Šoć, President of the Alliance of Electronic Sports of Montenegro and founder of Montenegro Future Festival, was also present. HRH Prince Gabriel of Nassau also expressed interest in the sector. The gathering also included Johann Strobl, former CEO of Raiffeisen Bank International, alongside entrepreneurs and leaders from other sectors. Their presence illustrated the event’s breadth beyond traditional cultural and royal circles. The inauguration of AVOR was a central moment of Castillia 2026. According to the official invitation, the international nonprofit initiative is developing a Global Cultural Atlas, with young people documenting the stories and places that shape cultural heritage. The launch took place on October 30 at Vienna Private Members, Wiener Rennverein, at Palais Esterházy. On October 31, the Heritage Salon was held at Park Hyatt Vienna, followed by the formal Heritage Gala at Palais Pallavicini. The programme concluded on November 1. The event was held under the patronage of figures including HRH Prince Omar bin Faisal of Jordan, Princess Ratsimamanga Ralandison Fenosoa, Maharani Sahiba Nivritti Kumari Mewar of Udaipur, Duke Michael zu Mecklenburg and Prince Constantin Sturdza. By bringing historic families, cultural leaders, business executives and figures from emerging industries into the same setting, Princess Sophia Wolkonsky’s Castillia offered a meeting point between the preservation of the past and the opportunities of the future. Disclosure: TNW CEO Alexandru Stan attended Castillia 2026 as a guest. TNW received no payment for this article, and Castillia did not cover travel or accommodation for our attendance.
