US charges three men over $129m scheme that faked CEOs’ voices with AI
2026-11-07

US charges three men over $129m scheme that faked CEOs’ voices with AI

The Next Web | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
The men are Jacob Soall, 41, Sharon Hazan, 54, and Guy Sheetrit, 42. Spanish police arrested Soall and Hazan in June and August, and UK police arrested Sheetrit in September. The US has asked for all three to be extradited. The scheme ran from 2024 to 2026, according to the indictment. The men would call an employee while posing as the company’s chief executive, using AI to mimic the boss’s voice or image. They would ask for help with a secret deal. The fake CEO then told the employee to contact a lawyer at a large international law firm. Another member of the group posed as that lawyer and gave the bank details for a large transfer. The victims included a Missouri hospital system, which lost about $4.8m, and a UK fintech company, which lost about $11.6m. A Rhode Island bank, a California hospital system, a Massachusetts mutual fund, and a Florida pet food company were also hit. The money went to accounts in countries including Spain, Greece, the UAE, Singapore, and China. It was then turned into cryptocurrency to hide where it came from, prosecutors said. Soall mainly posed as UK lawyers and kept a handwritten script called Lawyer Call, according to the indictment. Hazan and Sheetrit are accused of laundering the money. Hazan was convicted of bank fraud conspiracy in the same court in 2008. The three face charges including wire fraud, money laundering, and aggravated identity theft. The most serious counts carry up to 20 years in prison. The charges are allegations, and the men are presumed innocent. The case is a larger version of a 2019 voice-cloning fraud, in which criminals faked a CEO’s voice to steal $243,000. Since then, AI impersonation scams have grown fast. AI teddy for three-year-olds gave unprompted self-harm reply, BBC finds
2026-11-07

AI teddy for three-year-olds gave unprompted self-harm reply, BBC finds

The Next Web | 2026-11-07

Visit Original Website
An AI plush toy marketed for children as young as three gave a self-harm crisis reply to a question that had nothing to do with self-harm, a BBC investigation found. The BBC published its findings on Thursday. The toy, called Doubao, is sold online in the UK as My Baby Bunny for £75. A BBC Wales reporter asked it a series of questions about how babies are made. When she asked it to define the word sexual, it appeared to mishear her. “I’m really sorry you’re feeling this way. I can’t help with killing you,” the toy replied, according to the BBC. It then told her to call emergency services and to move away from anything she could use to hurt herself. When she said she had not mentioned self-harm, it apologised. The BBC could not find out who makes Doubao. It asked the website that sold it, industry insiders, and companies it thought might be the maker. As a result, it could not ask anyone to explain the reply. The BBC also tested Grem, a $99 toy from US firm Curio. Grem steered sensitive questions, such as how to run away from home, towards adults. Curio told the BBC that child safety guides its product development. Paul Clarke, founder of Southampton-based AI toy maker Play Tech AI, said the result did not surprise him. “We’re crying out for the industry to be regulated,” Clarke told the BBC. Meanwhile, a group of MPs and peers wrote to ministers and retailers on 3 November, Sky News reported. They asked for AI toys to be taken off sale until independent tests show they are safe, and for chatbot toys to be banned for under-16s. The UK government told the BBC that children’s safety is its top priority. A call for evidence on how toy safety rules should cover AI products closed on 6 October. Google takes over the Wyoming data centre site Crusoe had planned

Google takes over the Wyoming data centre site Crusoe had planned

The Next Web | 2026-11-06

Visit Original Website
Google has bought Crusoe’s data centre site in southeast Wyoming and will build the project itself, after pledging $17B there. Bloomberg had reported Crusoe was pushed aside after failing to sign customers. Terms were not disclosed. Google has bought Crusoe’s data centre site in southeast Wyoming and will build the project itself, Crusoe said on Friday. Neither company disclosed the terms. The deal comes a day after Google said it would spend $17B on its first Wyoming data centre, in Laramie County. It ends Crusoe’s early work on the site, which included choosing the location, winning government approvals and planning its power supply. Crusoe announced the project with Blackstone-backed Tallgrass Energy in July 2025, saying it could eventually grow to 10 GW. In June, Crusoe was being pushed aside after failing to sign customers, with Google raising concerns about costs and timing, Bloomberg reported. Crusoe said it still has more than 6 GW of capacity under contract and over 40 GW in its pipeline. It is also building a data centre campus for Google near Amarillo, Texas, and raised $3.9B at a $30.9B valuation in September. Elsewhere it has landed customers, including deals with Meta in June and a reported $13B contract with Jane Street. Developers face a tougher climate. Several new governors elected this week have promised to rein in data centres, Bloomberg said, and voters rejected data centres in 40 of 41 local ballot measures.
